{
  "abstract_verbatim": "We prove Ostmann's inverse Goldbach conjecture: no set differing from the primes by finitely many elements can be written as $`A+B`$, where <i>A</i> and <i>B</i> are sets of nonnegative integers with at least two elements each.",

<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove Ostmann's inverse Goldbach conjecture: no set differing from the primes by finitely many elements can be written as $`A+B`$, where <i>A</i> and <i>B</i> are sets of nonnegative integers with at least two elements each.

- **claim-ostmann-inverse-goldbach** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-ostmann)): If $A,B\subseteq\mathbb N_0$ satisfy $\|A\|,\|B\|\ge2$, then $(A+B)\mathbin{\triangle}\mathcal P$ is infinite. Equivalently, every finite modification of $\mathcal P$ is additively indecomposable into two sets with at least two elements each.
  - Explanation: No two sets with at least two nonnegative integers each have a sumset that differs from the primes by only finitely many elements.
- **claim-fixed-shifts-sieve** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-shifted-sieve)): Let $b_1,\ldots,b_j$ be distinct nonnegative integers, where $j\ge1$ is fixed. If $C\subset\mathbb N_0$ and each $c+b_i$ is prime for all sufficiently large $c\in C$, then $$C(Y)\ll_{b_1,\ldots,b_j} \frac{Y}{(\log Y)^j}.$$ The implicit constant depends only on the shifts; the threshold for $Y$ may also depend on the finite exceptional range.
  - Explanation: A set of integers all of whose translates by finitely many distinct fixed shifts are eventually prime has a counting function bounded by the length scale over a power of the logarithm equal to the number of shifts.
- **claim-finite-summands** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-factor)): If $A,B\subset\mathbb N_0$ have at least two elements each and $A+B$ agrees with $\mathcal P$ outside a finite set, then both $A$ and $B$ are infinite.
  - Explanation: If a sumset of two sets with at least two elements each agrees with the primes outside a finite set, then neither summand can be finite.
- **claim-two-infinite-summands** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There do not exist two infinite sets $A,B\subseteq\mathbb N_0$ such that $(A+B)\mathbin{\triangle}\mathcal P$ is finite.
  - Explanation: The technical core of the paper: two infinite sets cannot have a sumset that agrees with the primes up to a finite symmetric difference.
- **claim-square-root-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-size)): Under the assumed decomposition with both summands infinite, $$\label{eq:src1} \frac{\sqrt Y}{(\log Y)^3}\ll A(Y),B(Y) \ll \sqrt Y(\log Y)^2.$$
  - Explanation: Under the assumed decomposition both counting functions are squeezed between the square root of the length scale divided by a power of the logarithm and the square root times a power of the logarithm.
- **claim-collision-stability** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-collision)): Let $b\ge1$ be fixed. Suppose $\mu,\nu$ are probability measures on $$A\cap(\sqrt X+N_*,X],\qquad D\cap[-X,-\sqrt X-N_*),$$ respectively, and that every point mass is at most $(\log X)^b/\sqrt X$. Put $Q=\sqrt X/(\log X)^{b+1}$. Then $$\label{eq:src2} \begin{split} \sum_{p\le Q}\log p\bigg( &\\|\mu_p-U_{S_p}\\|_2^2+\\|\nu_p-U_{S_p^c}\\|_2^2\\ &+\frac{\sigma_p^{-1}+(1-\sigma_p)^{-1}-4}{p} \bigg)\ll_b\log\log X. \end{split}$$ All terms on the left are nonnegative.
  - Explanation: For measures on the two tails with small point masses, a logarithmically weighted sum over small primes of the squared distances to uniform measures on the complementary residue supports, plus an imbalance term, is bounded by the iterated logarithm of the scale.
- **claim-quadratic-decorrelation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-quadratic)): Under the assumed eventual decomposition of the primes, $$\label{eq:src3} \sum_{T\leq \log p\leq 2T}\frac{\log p}{p} \max_{t\in\mathbb F_p} \left\|\mathbb E_{x\in S_p}\chi_p(x-t)\right\|=o(T) \qquad (T\longrightarrow\infty).$$
  - Explanation: On dyadic prime bands the logarithmically weighted maximal bias of the residue support against any translated quadratic character is negligible compared with the band parameter.
- **claim-quadratic-harmonic** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-quad-harmonic)): For fixed $0<\alpha<\beta$, as $L_*\longrightarrow\infty$, $$\sum_{\alpha L_*\leq\log\log p\leq\beta L_*}\frac1p \max_{t\in\mathbb F_p} \left\|\mathbb E_{x\in S_p}\chi_p(x-t)\right\|=o(L_*).$$
  - Explanation: The quadratic decorrelation is restated for harmonic weights over double-logarithmic prime bands.
- **claim-translated-characters** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-characters)): For every fixed $0<\alpha<\beta$, one has $$\label{eq:src16} \sum_{\alpha L\le \log\log p\le\beta L}\frac1p \max_{\substack{t\in\mathbb F_p,\ \lambda\ {\rm multiplicative}\\ \mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(\lambda)>2}} \left\|\mathbb E_{x\in S_p}\lambda(x-t)\right\|=o(L) \qquad(L\longrightarrow\infty).$$
  - Explanation: For multiplicative characters of order greater than two, translated correlations with the residue support are negligible in harmonic mass over double-logarithmic bands.
- **claim-mixed-flatness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-mixed-flatness)): Define $$F_p(x)=\frac{\mathbf 1_{S_p}(x)-\sigma_p} {\sqrt{\sigma_p(1-\sigma_p)}}, \qquad g_p(v)=\sqrt p\,\mathbb E_{x\in\mathbb F_p}F_p(x)e_p(-vx).$$ For every fixed $0<\alpha<\beta$, outside a set of primes of harmonic mass $o(L)$ in $\alpha L\le\log\log p\le\beta L$, one has uniformly $$\label{eq:src27} \sigma_p=\tfrac12+o(1),\qquad \max_{\substack{a\in\mathbb F_p\\\lambda\ {\rm multiplicative}}} \left\|\mathbb E_{v\in\mathbb F_p}g_p(v)\lambda(v)e_p(av)\right\|=o(1).$$
  - Explanation: Outside a negligible set of primes the residue densities are balanced and the character-twisted shifted transforms of the centred indicator are uniformly small.
- **claim-nonsparse-supply** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-supply)): There is an absolute constant $\delta_0>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $L$, $$\label{eq:src28} \sum_{\substack{.05L\le \log\log p\le .9L\\ 1/3\le \sigma_p\le2/3\\ \gamma_p\ge\delta_0}} \frac1p \ \ge\ .15L .$$
  - Explanation: A fixed positive proportion of harmonic prime mass in the band consists of balanced primes whose normalised additive transform has non-small first-power mean.
- **claim-local-contraction** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-supply-local-contraction)): For sufficiently small $\varepsilon_0$, uniformly for $p\in\mathcal S$ and complex $u,v$ with $\|u\|,\|v\|\le1.03$, $$\\|L_p(u,v)\\|\le1+C/p,\qquad \|U_p(u,v)\|\le1+C/p.$$ For all sufficiently large such primes, $$\label{eq:src31} \\|L_p(1,1)\\|\le U_p(1,1)(1-1.6/p), \qquad U_p(1,1)=1+O(\varepsilon_0/p).$$ All constants are absolute.
  - Explanation: The local operator and unit-mean kernels stay bounded by a factor close to one, and the operator norm at the base point is strictly smaller than the unit mean by a relative amount of order one over the prime.
- **claim-total-prime-error** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-supply-total-prime-error)): Fix $C_K>0$, and let $\phi$ be a fixed smooth function compactly supported in $(1/2,1)$. Among primitive characters of conductor at most $Q_b$, omit the possible exceptional character in the Landau–Page theorem. Include the conductor-$1$ principal character. For every fixed $D_1>0$, $$\label{eq:src33} \sum_{\chi}^{\mathrm{nonexc}} \left\| \sum_{n\ge1}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)\phi(n/X) -\mathbf 1_{\chi=1}X\int_0^\infty\phi(t)\,dt \right\| \ll_{D_1,\phi,C_K}Xe^{-D_1L}.$$
  - Explanation: Summed over the non-exceptional primitive characters up to the conductor bound, the smoothed prime sums deviate from the principal main term by an arbitrarily small power of the exponential of the large parameter.
- **claim-zero-free-refinement** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-auto-remark-1)): Theorem 1.1 of (OpenAI 2026) [[OpenAIQRH2026](#ref-OpenAIQRH2026)] implies that <a href="#eq:src33" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src33">[eq:src33]</a> also holds when the sum includes *all* primitive characters of conductor at most $Q_b$, including the conductor-$1$ principal character. Indeed, every nontrivial zero then has real part at most $7/8$. The smoothed explicit formula <a href="#eq:supply-smoothed-explicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:supply-smoothed-explicit">[eq:supply-smoothed-explicit]</a>, the rapid decay of $\Phi$, and the zero-count bound used above give an error $O_\phi(X^{7/8}\log(2q))$ for each primitive character of conductor $q$; for the conductor-$1$ character, subtract the pole main term. There are $O(Q_b^2)$ such characters, so their total error is $O_\phi(Q_b^2X^{7/8}\log(2Q_b))$, and $$Q_b^2X^{7/8}\log(2Q_b)=X^{7/8+o(1)} \ll_{D_1,C_K}Xe^{-D_1L}$$ for every fixed $D_1>0$, since $K=\lceil C_KL\rceil$ and $\log Q_b=o(\log X)$. The proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:supply" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:supply">11</a>, including its exceptional-character deletion, uses the classical estimate of Lemma <a href="#lem:supply-total-prime-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:supply-total-prime-error">13</a>, not this zero-free theorem.
  - Explanation: An external zero-free half-plane result lets the character sum estimate include every primitive character, with no deletion of an exceptional one.
- **claim-tree-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-tree-comparison)): In a single diagram let the leaf variables be independent uniform elements of $U$. Then $$\label{eq:src37} \mathbb E\|W\|^2\leq3^r.$$ For two diagrams of the same depth, partition the leaves in each into $b$ nonempty sets, with the sets in the two diagrams paired. Give the two collections of leaf variables the uniform distribution on the subgroup specified by equality of the products on each paired set. The diagrams may have different unit parameters and different conjugation choices. If $l\geq2$ and $b\leq3r/4$, then under <a href="#eq:src35" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src35">[eq:src35]</a>, uniformly in these choices, $$\label{eq:src38} \|\mathbb EW_1\overline{W_2}\|=o_l(1).$$ More precisely, the left side is at most $C_l(\varepsilon_q+q^{-1/4})$ for a constant depending only on $l$.
  - Explanation: A single diagram has bounded second moment, and two diagrams whose leaf products are tied together with few overlap components are asymptotically uncorrelated, using only mixed-character smallness of the transform.
- **claim-comparison-estimates** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-comparisons)): In either of the two outer environments, the following estimates hold uniformly over the indicated assignments. For one assignment, $$\mathbb E\sum_{0<\|s\|\le V_l}\mathcal G\|A_l(s)\|^2 \le\exp\bigl(r(\Delta_0+Cm)+o_k(m)\bigr). \label{eq:src55}$$ The constant $C$ is independent of sufficiently large $k$ and of the fixed gap budgets. For a pair of assignments, if $l\ge2$ and its overlap graph has at most $3r/4$ connected components, then $$\left\|\mathbb E\sum_{0<\|s\|\le V_l}\mathcal G K_{\rm ar} A_l^{(1)}(s)\overline{A_l^{(2)}(s)}\right\| \le\exp(-\omega_k(m)). \label{eq:src56}$$ Here $\omega_k(m)/m\to\infty$ as $L\to\infty$ for every fixed choice of the other parameters. Equivalently, the right side of <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a> is smaller than $\exp(-C_km)$ for every fixed $C_k$ once $L$ is sufficiently large. The estimate is uniform in the allowed slot matchings and permutations.
  - Explanation: In both outer environments a single assignment has an exponentially bounded weighted second moment, while pairs of assignments with few overlap components have a correlation that decays faster than any exponential in the bulk parameter.
- **claim-prime-progression** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-arith-progression)): Fix a row of <a href="#eq:arith-progression-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-progression-scales">[eq:arith-progression-scales]</a>. There is at most one primitive real character $\chi_*$, with conductor at most $\exp(\exp(\mu L))$, and one associated real zero $\beta_*<1$ which must be retained in the following formula. If $$\log M_*\leq e^{\mu L},\qquad (a,M_*)=1,$$ and $I$ is an interval of length at most one in $[e^{a_0L},e^{a_1L}]$, then $$\label{eq:src57} \sum_{\substack{p\ \text{prime}\,,\ \log p\in I\\p\equiv a\pmod{M_*}}} \frac1p =\frac1{\phi(M_*)}\int_I \bigl(1-\chi_*(a)e^{(\beta_*-1)t}\bigr)\frac{dt}{t} +O\bigl(\exp(-c e^{\delta'L})\bigr).$$ The exceptional term is omitted if no such character exists or its conductor does not divide $M_*$. The implied constant is absolute for the fixed row. The multiplier inside the integral lies in $[0,2]$.
  - Explanation: Reciprocal prime sums over short logarithmic intervals in a residue class equal the expected main integral with a possible exceptional real-zero correction, up to a doubly exponentially small error.

        "exact_quote": "Fix $C_K>0$, and let $\\phi$ be a fixed smooth function compactly supported in $(1/2,1)$. Among primitive characters of conductor at most $Q_b$, omit the possible exceptional character in the Landau–Page theorem. Include the conductor-$1$ principal character. For every fixed $D_1>0$, $$\\label{eq:src33}\n \\sum_{\\chi}^{\\mathrm{nonexc}}\n \\left|\n   \\sum_{n\\ge1}\\Lambda(n)\\chi(n)\\phi(n/X)\n       -\\mathbf 1_{\\chi=1}X\\int_0^\\infty\\phi(t)\\,dt\n \\right|\n \\ll_{D_1,\\phi,C_K}Xe^{-D_1L}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-013.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-auto-remark-1",
        "exact_quote": "Theorem 1.1 of (OpenAI 2026) [[OpenAIQRH2026](#ref-OpenAIQRH2026)] implies that <a href=\"#eq:src33\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:src33\">[eq:src33]</a> also holds when the sum includes *all* primitive characters of conductor at most $Q_b$, including the conductor-$1$ principal character. Indeed, every nontrivial zero then has real part at most $7/8$. The smoothed explicit formula <a href=\"#eq:supply-smoothed-explicit\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:supply-smoothed-explicit\">[eq:supply-smoothed-explicit]</a>, the rapid decay of $\\Phi$, and the zero-count bound used above give an error $O_\\phi(X^{7/8}\\log(2q))$ for each primitive character of conductor $q$; for the conductor-$1$ character, subtract the pole main term. There are $O(Q_b^2)$ such characters, so their total error is $O_\\phi(Q_b^2X^{7/8}\\log(2Q_b))$, and $$Q_b^2X^{7/8}\\log(2Q_b)=X^{7/8+o(1)}\n   \\ll_{D_1,C_K}Xe^{-D_1L}$$ for every fixed $D_1>0$, since $K=\\lceil C_KL\\rceil$ and $\\log Q_b=o(\\log X)$. There are $O(Q_b^2)$ such characters, so their total error is $O_\\phi(Q_b^2X^{7/8}\\log(2Q_b))$, and $$Q_b^2X^{7/8}\\log(2Q_b)=X^{7/8+o(1)}\n   \\ll_{D_1,C_K}Xe^{-D_1L}$$ for every fixed $D_1>0$, since $K=\\lceil C_KL\\rceil$ and $\\log Q_b=o(\\log X)$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-tree-comparison",
        "exact_quote": "In a single diagram let the leaf variables be independent uniform elements of $U$. Then $$\\label{eq:src37}\n                       \\mathbb E|W|^2\\leq3^r.$$ For two diagrams of the same depth, partition the leaves in each into $b$ nonempty sets, with the sets in the two diagrams paired. Give the two collections of leaf variables the uniform distribution on the subgroup specified by equality of the products on each paired set. The diagrams may have different unit parameters and different conjugation choices. If $l\\geq2$ and $b\\leq3r/4$, then under <a href=\"#eq:src35\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:src35\">[eq:src35]</a>, uniformly in these choices, $$\\label{eq:src38}\n                         |\\mathbb EW_1\\overline{W_2}|=o_l(1).$$ More precisely, the left side is at most $C_l(\\varepsilon_q+q^{-1/4})$ for a constant depending only on $l$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **positive primes** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Let $\mathcal P$ denote the set of positive primes, and put $\mathbb N_0=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$.
- **sumset** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For $A,B\subseteq\mathbb N_0$, write $A+B=\{a+b:a\in A,\ b\in B\}$.
- **asymptotically equal** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Two subsets of $\mathbb N_0$ are *asymptotically equal* if their symmetric difference is finite.
- **counting function** ([source](#sec:preliminaries)): For $C\subseteq\mathbb N_0$, put $C(Y)=\|C\cap[0,Y]\|$.
- **reflected summand** ([source](#eq:eventual-equality)): Choose an integer $N_*>\|N\|+10$ and put $D=-B$.
- **complementary residue supports** ([source](#pm-h-6)): Choose a partition $S_p,S_p^c$ of $\mathbb F_p$ containing the respective sets, and write $\sigma_p=\|S_p\|/p$.
- **uniform measure on a finite set** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Write $U_E$ for uniform probability on a finite nonempty set $E$.
- **projection of a measure modulo p** ([source](#pm-h-8)): If $\mu$ is a probability measure on integers, write $\mu_p$ for its projection modulo $p$.
- **harmonic mass** ([source](#sec:characters)): In this section the harmonic mass of a set of primes $\mathcal P$ is $\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-1}$.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-upper-count-bound | A(Y), B(Y) | ≤ | $\sqrt Y(\log Y)^2$ | Implied constant is absolute up to the fixed data; large Y |
| q-lower-count-bound | A(Y), B(Y) | ≥ | $\frac{\sqrt Y}{(\log Y)^3}$ | Implied constant fixed; large Y |
| q-shifted-sieve-count | C(Y) | ≤ | $\frac{Y}{(\log Y)^j}$ | Implied constant depends only on the shifts |
| q-bias-scale-exponent | \rho | = | $\frac35$ | Fixed exponent linking X and T |
| q-moment-exponent | \mu_0 | = | $10^{-6}$ | Fixed exponent controlling the moment order |
| q-prime-block-exponent | \gamma | = | $10^{-7}$ | Fixed exponent for the auxiliary prime block |
| q-supply-harmonic-mass | harmonic mass of balanced primes with non-small transform mean | ≥ | $.15L$ | Absolute constant delta_0; large L |
| q-sparse-prime-mass | H | ≥ | $.68L$ | Valid after deleting one prime of the sparse set |
| q-tree-second-moment | \mathbb E\|W\|^2 | ≤ | $3^r$ | Field size large; r equals a power of two |
| q-giant-normalisation | c_g | ≤ | $.91L$ | Sufficiently large L |
| q-scale-h | h | = | $e^{.01L}$ | Depth k fixed before L tends to infinity |
| q-final-lower-bound | \|\eta_k\|^2 | ≥ | $\exp(-2Brm)$ | Constants fixed first, then k, then L large |
| q-final-upper-bound | \|\eta_k\|^2 | ≤ | $\exp(-(2B+1)rm)$ | Sufficiently large L at fixed constants and depth |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-shifted-sieve** [claim](#stmt-lem-shifted-sieve) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-shifted-sieve): Additive large sieve applied to the avoided classes modulo primes, with Parseval and Cauchy–Schwarz, then Mertens estimates and Markov's inequality. -> counting bound for sets with several eventually prime shifts
- **s-finite-factor** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-factor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-factor): Apply the shifted sieve to a finite summand and compare with the coverage lower bound. -> both summands are infinite
- **s-size** [claim](#stmt-lem-size) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-size): Large sieve on primes in a window, collision probability of an independent pair, and the prime number theorem. -> square-root bounds for both counting functions
- **s-collision** [claim](#stmt-lem-collision) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-collision): Unequal-pair count and Mertens estimate for collision energy against uniform measure on the support. -> weighted collision stability
- **s-quadratic** [claim](#stmt-prop-quadratic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-quadratic): Amplified moment, Poisson summation, hypercontractive moment bound, Dirichlet approximation, quadratic large sieve and a collision contradiction. -> negligible bias against translated quadratic characters
- **s-quad-harmonic** [claim](#stmt-cor-quad-harmonic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-quad-harmonic): Sum the dyadic-band estimate over the range. -> harmonic-weighted quadratic decorrelation
- **s-characters** [claim](#stmt-prop-characters) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-characters): Repeated Cauchy–Schwarz transfers with anchor primes, frequency histories, one-sided character cancellation, permutation averaging. -> negligible bias against translated higher-order characters
- **s-mixed-flatness** [claim](#stmt-cor-mixed-flatness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-mixed-flatness): Gauss sum formula reducing twisted transforms to translated character averages, then Markov's inequality. -> balance and mixed-character flatness
- **s-supply-kernel** [claim](#stmt-lem-supply-local-contraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-supply-local-contraction): Block-matrix norm analysis of the local kernel on centred coordinates. -> local contraction of the operator norm
- **s-supply-prime-error** [claim](#stmt-lem-supply-total-prime-error) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-supply-total-prime-error): Zero-density theorem, zero-free region with Landau–Page, and smoothed explicit formula. -> small total error for smoothed prime sums
- **s-supply** [claim](#stmt-prop-supply) / [proof](#pm-h-33): Contradiction: tensor kernel upper bound against a coverage lower bound for a nonnegative weight. -> harmonic mass of balanced primes with non-sparse transforms
- **s-tree** [claim](#stmt-lem-tree-comparison) / [proof](#pm-h-36): Density domination, cycle reduction in the overlap multigraph, quartet autocorrelation estimates and Mellin–Fourier bounds. -> tree comparison estimate
- **s-arith-progression** [claim](#stmt-lem-arith-progression) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-arith-progression): Zero-free region with exceptional zero retained, smoothed explicit formula and sandwiching of the interval. -> prime sums in progressions with exceptional term
- **s-comparisons** [claim](#stmt-prop-comparisons) / [proof](#sec:arithmetic): Idealize primes by real coordinates and unit residues, remove coincidences, then apply the tree comparison at spectators and sum frequencies. -> norm bound and paired decay estimate
- **s-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-62): Transfer recursion lower bound against a symmetrization upper bound by Cauchy–Schwarz, giving a contradiction. -> no two infinite summands
- **s-ostmann** [claim](#stmt-cor-ostmann) / [proof](#pm-h-5): Combine finite-summand reduction with the two-infinite-summand theorem. -> Ostmann's conjecture

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-two-infinite-summands-impossible**: Under the standing hypothesis that two infinite sets of nonnegative integers had a sumset differing from the primes in only finitely many places, a contradiction follows; hence no such pair of infinite sets exists.
- **inv-ostmann-two-element-summands**: For any two subsets of the nonnegative integers with at least two elements each, the symmetric difference of their sumset with the primes is infinite.
- **inv-summands-must-be-infinite**: If a sumset of two sets with at least two elements each agrees with the primes outside a finite set, then both summands are infinite.
- **inv-square-root-size-bounds**: Under the assumed decomposition with both summands infinite, the counting functions of both summands lie between a square-root scale divided by a power of the logarithm and a square-root scale times a power of the logarithm.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-finite-range-tables**: Conclude that no additive factorisation of a finite table of primes (a bounded range or fixed-width integer window) into two non-trivial sets can exist. -> The result is purely asymptotic: it says an infinite symmetric difference occurs, and says nothing about truncated ranges, where sumset representations of prime tables may well exist.
- **anti-direct-goldbach-solver**: Use the theorem as a tool for representing integers as sums of two primes. -> The theorem asks whether the prime set itself is a sumset; primes play the role of the sum, not of the summands, so it gives no representation of integers by primes.
- **anti-unconditional-size-bounds**: Quote the square-root growth bounds for the counting functions as unconditional facts about arbitrary additive factors of prime-like sets. -> The bounds are derived only under the assumed decomposition of the primes with both summands infinite; they are consequences inside a proof by contradiction, and no such sets exist by the main theorem.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The paper's results are non-existence statements proved by contradiction: no pair of infinite sets with the stated sumset property exists. The argument is asymptotic, passes to unbounded limits, and relies on unspecified sufficiently large constants and on analytic estimates for primes and Dirichlet characters. No finite procedure, explicit construction or computable output is stated whose correctness could be run against an independent oracle.

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an asymptotic impossibility for infinite sets of integers. It states no procedure with inputs and outputs that a program could run, so it supplies no library or API contract. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper models a dynamical system, an environment or an agent. The result is a statement about symmetric differences of infinite sumsets and gives nothing to simulate. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem is a negative existence statement, so it yields no usable utility. Ruling out a decomposition gives a tool nothing to compute, check or transform. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proof is a contradiction argument built on analytic estimates with asymptotic constants. It contains no finite constructive procedure to implement, so no pseudocode or complexity claim is justified. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The sets $A$ and $B$ are hypothetical infinite objects assumed only to be refuted. The paper defines no representation, index or storage layout for them. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no fixed finite kernel or dataflow here. The estimates hold only as the large parameters tend to infinity, so nothing can be mapped to a circuit or accelerator. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result shows that the primes have no cofinite additive factorisation. It gives no hardness assumption, reduction or security parameter for any scheme. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper concerns no data, loss or learning model. Its character-sum decorrelation estimates are tools for the contradiction and are not training or generalisation statements. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no objective function or feasible set to optimise. The final step compares an inductive lower bound with an upper bound to reach a contradiction, not a solution of an optimisation problem. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No nodes, messages, faults or consensus appear. The tree diagrams are algebraic bookkeeping over finite-field variables and do not model communicating processes. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No further engineering use is supported. The only transferable value is mathematical: it fixes in additive number theory that the primes cannot be refined into a sum of two sets with at least two elements each. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-asymptotic-scope-only** (formal_scope): The conclusion concerns the infinite set of primes: the symmetric difference of the sumset with the primes is infinite. It does not exclude a sumset representation of the primes inside any bounded range or truncated table. Blocked use: ['concluding that a finite or fixed-width table of primes has no additive factorisation into two non-trivial sets', 'using the theorem as a certificate for a bounded computation']
- **limit-two-element-summands** (hypothesis): Both summands must be subsets of the nonnegative integers with at least two elements, and the reduction to infinite summands holds only when the sumset agrees with the primes outside a finite set. Blocked use: ['applying the result to a singleton summand, which is only a translate of the other set', 'applying the result to sets that are not subsets of the nonnegative integers']
- **limit-coverage-and-exclusion-both** (hypothesis): The conclusion needs both eventual coverage of the primes and eventual exclusion of composite sums. A density condition replaces neither requirement. Blocked use: ['replacing the exact prime coverage by a positive-density or counting-function condition', 'dropping the exclusion of composite sums from the standing hypothesis']
- **limit-proof-by-contradiction** (nonconstructive): The main theorem is proved by assuming that two infinite sets with the stated eventual equality exist and deriving a contradiction. It yields no finite procedure and no explicit object. Blocked use: ['extracting an algorithm, search procedure or construction from the proof', 'reading intermediate estimates as properties of real objects rather than consequences of an assumed decomposition']
- **limit-size-bounds-conditional** (counterexample_scope): The square-root bounds for the counting functions are derived only under the assumed decomposition with both summands infinite. The main theorem shows that no such sets exist. Blocked use: ['quoting the growth bounds as unconditional facts about arbitrary additive factors', 'using the bounds outside the proof by contradiction']
- **limit-fixed-data-implied-constants** (approximation): Implied constants and thresholds depend on the fixed data of the assumed decomposition, and parameters are fixed in a stated order before the large scale tends to infinity. The estimates give no explicit numerical range of validity. Blocked use: ['reading the asymptotic bounds as explicit inequalities at a given finite scale', 'reordering or jointly varying the depth and the large parameter']
- **limit-siegel-ineffective** (nonconstructive): The treatment of exceptional characters uses Siegel's theorem only for existence and asymptotics. No effective constant is available, so no effective threshold can be read off. Blocked use: ['deriving an explicit numerical size beyond which the argument applies', 'claiming an effective version of the decorrelation estimates']
- **limit-zero-free-remark-optional** (formal_scope): The zero-free half-plane result enters only an optional remark. The main proof uses the classical zero-free region and the Landau–Page theorem, so the proof does not depend on the external theorem. Blocked use: ['treating the external zero-free theorem as a prerequisite of the main result', 'dropping the exceptional-character deletion in the main proof on the strength of the remark']

> The formalization proves Ostmann's inverse Goldbach conjecture. For any two sets $A,B$ of nonnegative integers, each containing at least two elements, the symmetric difference between their sumset $A+B$ and the set of primes is infinite. Thus no set differing from the primes by only finitely many elements can be decomposed as such a sumset. The linked statements include the full result and its two-infinite-summands case.

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove Ostmann's inverse Goldbach conjecture: no set differing from the primes by finitely many elements can be written as $`A+B`$, where <i>A</i> and <i>B</i> are sets of nonnegative integers with at least two elements each.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $\mathcal P$ denote the set of positive primes, and put $\mathbb N_0=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. For $A,B\subseteq\mathbb N_0$, write $A+B=\{a+b:a\in A,\ b\in B\}$. Two subsets of $\mathbb N_0$ are *asymptotically equal* if their symmetric difference is finite. Ostmann’s conjecture asserts that $\mathcal P$ is asymptotically additively indecomposable: it is not asymptotically equal to $A+B$ when both summands contain at least two elements. The conjecture goes back to his 1956 treatise (Ostmann 1956, 7:13) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Ostmann1956](#ref-Ostmann1956)]; the eventual-equality formulation and its terminology are recorded explicitly in (Elsholtz and Harper 2015, Definition 1.1 and Conjecture 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[ElsholtzHarper](#ref-ElsholtzHarper)]. It is also known as the inverse Goldbach problem.

<a id="stmt-cor-ostmann"></a>
<div id="cor:ostmann" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Ostmann’s conjecture). *If $A,B\subseteq\mathbb N_0$ satisfy $|A|,|B|\ge2$, then $(A+B)\mathbin{\triangle}\mathcal P$ is infinite. Equivalently, every finite modification of $\mathcal P$ is additively indecomposable into two sets with at least two elements each.*

</div>

Laffer and Mann showed that a hypothetical decomposition must have two infinite summands (Laffer and Mann 1964, Theorem 12) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[LafferMann1964](#ref-LafferMann1964)]. We first give a short sieve proof of this reduction in Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-factor">3</a>, then state the two-infinite-set contradiction as Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">4</a>. The rest of the paper proves that theorem.

The conclusion concerns both eventual coverage of the primes and eventual exclusion of composite sums. Neither requirement is replaced by a density condition. In particular, if $|A|,|B|\ge2$ and $A+B$ contains every sufficiently large prime, then it contains infinitely many composite numbers.

Earlier work established increasingly strong restrictions on a hypothetical decomposition. Hornfeck’s work (Hornfeck 1954, 1955) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Hornfeck1954](#ref-Hornfeck1954), [Hornfeck1955](#ref-Hornfeck1955)] was followed by sieve arguments of Pomerance, Sárközy and Stewart (Pomerance, Sárközy, and Stewart 1988) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[PomeranceSarkozyStewart1988](#ref-PomeranceSarkozyStewart1988)], Hofmann and Wolke (Hofmann and Wolke 1996) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[HofmannWolke1996](#ref-HofmannWolke1996)], and Elsholtz (Elsholtz 2001a) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Elsholtz2001Remark](#ref-Elsholtz2001Remark)]. Write $A(x)=|A\cap[0,x]|$ and similarly for $B$. Bounds such as $A(x)B(x)\ll x$ are compatible with the coverage lower bound $A(x)B(x)\gg x/\log x$ and therefore do not by themselves exclude a decomposition. Elsholtz combined the large and larger sieves to put both counting functions at the square-root scale, up to powers of $\log x$, and ruled out a decomposition into three nontrivial summands (Elsholtz 2001b) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Elsholtz2001](#ref-Elsholtz2001)]. His later refinement (Elsholtz 2006, Theorem 1.9) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Elsholtz2006](#ref-Elsholtz2006)] gives the bounds reproduced in Lemma <a href="#lem:size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:size">5</a>. Croot and Elsholtz also studied thin ternary sumsets contained in the primes, obtaining restrictions under a regularity hypothesis on representation multiplicities (E. S. Croot III and Elsholtz 2005, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[CrootElsholtz2005](#ref-CrootElsholtz2005)]. Shao later proved a finite ternary obstruction: for some absolute $c>0$, three subsets of $[1,N]$, each of size at least $N^{1/3-c}$, have a composite number in their sumset once $N$ is sufficiently large (Shao 2016, Theorem 1.3 of the preprint version) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Shao2016](#ref-Shao2016)]. In their positive-integer formulation, Elsholtz and Harper sharpened the binary counting bounds to $$\frac{\sqrt x}{\log x\log\log x}
 \ll A(x),B(x)\ll\sqrt x\log\log x$$ under a hypothetical eventual decomposition (Elsholtz and Harper 2015, Theorem 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[ElsholtzHarper](#ref-ElsholtzHarper)].

Green and Harper developed inverse questions for the large sieve and proved that a suitable inverse-sieve conjecture would imply Ostmann’s conjecture (Green and Harper 2014, Conjecture 1.5 and Theorem 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GreenHarper](#ref-GreenHarper)]. Their conjecture proposes a quadratic description of sets near the square-root sieve bound. Under half-residue restrictions $|C\bmod p|\le p/2+O(1)$ at every prime and $|C|\gg\sqrt N$ for $C\subseteq[1,N]$, Hanson proved additive correlation with the squares and a logarithmic-size intersection with a quadratic image (Hanson 2020, Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Hanson2020](#ref-Hanson2020)]. Croot, Mao and Yip subsequently proved inverse theorems for local restrictions to short arithmetic progressions, with extensions to unions of progressions and other additive structures (E. Croot, Mao, and Yip 2025) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[CrootMaoYip2025](#ref-CrootMaoYip2025)]. More recently, Croot, Mao, Pohoata and Yip used a weighted entropy argument to derive a further necessary condition for a hypothetical decomposition of the primes into two sets of positive integers, each containing at least two elements (E. Croot et al. 2026, Theorem 1.10 and Corollary 1.11) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CrootMaoPohoataYip2026](#ref-CrootMaoPohoataYip2026)]. For some $c>0$ and every sufficiently large $N$, each summand then has at least $\exp(c\sqrt{\log N}/(\log\log N)^{3/2})$ elements in $[1,N]$ lying in an integral quadratic image $m\pm\mathbb Z^2$; the product of the two intersection sizes is at least $\exp(c\sqrt{\log N}/\log\log N)$. These quadratics may depend on $N$.

These large intersections with quadratic images fall short of the near-containment required by Green and Harper’s conditional route. Our proof uses the simultaneous residue restrictions and coverage of every sufficiently large prime to reach a contradiction without such a classification.

Under a hypothetical decomposition, put $D=-B$. For each prime $p$, deleting finite initial segments from $A$ and $B$ leaves disjoint images of $A$ and $D$ in $\mathbb F_p$. Completing these subsets to a partition gives a residue set $S_p$ and its complement. The collision estimate in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> shows, on suitable prime averages, that the two parts have approximately equal size and that the summands are approximately uniform on their respective parts.

The next stage rules out persistent correlations with translated multiplicative characters. Section <a href="#sec:quadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:quadratic">3</a> treats quadratic characters with independently chosen translating centers at every prime. A moment argument and Poisson summation force a common rational center. The quadratic large sieve then confines positive fractions of long tails of the summands to a small family of quadratic kernels, leading to a collision contradiction. Section <a href="#sec:characters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:characters">4</a> treats all higher character orders by repeated Cauchy–Schwarz transfers. Its anchor variables distinguish the copied terms and supply the character cancellation needed to control the diagonals. Together these arguments give the mixed-character decorrelation in Corollary <a href="#cor:mixed-flatness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:mixed-flatness">10</a>.

Prime coverage enters separately in Section <a href="#sec:supply" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:supply">5</a>. A tensor estimate shows that the normalized additive transforms of the residue indicators cannot have small $L^1$ norm on too much harmonic prime mass. The proof compares a nonnegative sumset statistic with its average over the primes. This comparison retains the coverage information beyond the preliminary lower bounds for the summands.

The remaining residue indicators need not have a prescribed algebraic form. Section <a href="#sec:tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tree">6</a> proves a finite-field comparison for the binary trees produced by the transfers, using the mixed-character decorrelation. Its hypotheses involve only a probability $L^2$ bound and mixed-character correlations; they permit highly nonuniform pointwise values. This feature is needed for the exact Fourier transforms of the residue indicators. Sections <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">7</a> and <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">8</a> construct a positive statistic and establish its comparison estimates. The arithmetic separation includes repeated internal prime labels, coprimality conditions, and the possible exceptional real character in prime progression estimates. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">9</a> averages over permutations of the bulk variables. Most pairs of arrangements have few overlap components and hence small correlation. Their total contribution and the remaining diagonal terms contradict the lower bound inherited from the positive statistic. The transfer depth is chosen sufficiently large and fixed before the large scale tends to infinity.

This proves asymptotic indecomposability directly from the simultaneous residue restrictions and prime coverage. The argument does not require the general inverse-sieve conjecture of (Green and Harper 2014) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GreenHarper](#ref-GreenHarper)], and no such general classification theorem is asserted here.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Finite summands and residue supports

For $C\subseteq\mathbb N_0$, put $C(Y)=|C\cap[0,Y]|$. All logarithms are natural.

We use $e(x)=\exp(2\pi i x)$ and $e_m(x)=e(x/m)$. An expectation over a finite set means its uniform probability average unless another probability law is specified. Multiplicative characters, including the principal character, are extended by zero at zero. For functions on $\mathbb F_p$ our Fourier convention is $$\widehat f(a)=\frac1p\sum_{x\in\mathbb F_p}f(x)e_p(-ax),
 \qquad
 f(x)=\sum_{a\in\mathbb F_p}\widehat f(a)e_p(ax).$$ Norms of functions on a finite field use probability measure. A mass function $\mu$ of a probability measure instead uses the counting norm $\|\mu\|_2^2=\sum_x|\mu(x)|^2$ when explicitly so stated below. This distinction is useful in the collision estimate.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### A sieve for finitely many shifts

<a id="eq:prelim-large-sieve"></a>
We use the classical additive large sieve in the following form. If complex coefficients $c_n$ are supported on an interval of $M$ consecutive integers and $Q\ge1$, then $$\label{eq:prelim-large-sieve}
 \sum_{q\le Q}\ \sum_{\substack{h\bmod q\\(h,q)=1}}
 \left|\sum_n c_n e_q(hn)\right|^2
 \ll (M+Q^2)\sum_n|c_n|^2.$$ Indeed, the reduced fractions with denominators at most $Q$ are $Q^{-2}$-separated modulo one, so this is the separated-frequency inequality of Montgomery and Vaughan (H. L. Montgomery and Vaughan 1973, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan1973](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan1973)]; see also Green and Harper (Green and Harper 2014, Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[GreenHarper](#ref-GreenHarper)]. The estimate applies to arbitrary complex coefficients. Restricting its outer sum to prime or squarefree moduli is permitted by nonnegativity.

<a id="stmt-lem-shifted-sieve"></a>
<div id="lem:shifted-sieve" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Fixed shifts). *Let $b_1,\ldots,b_j$ be distinct nonnegative integers, where $j\ge1$ is fixed. If $C\subset\mathbb N_0$ and each $c+b_i$ is prime for all sufficiently large $c\in C$, then $$C(Y)\ll_{b_1,\ldots,b_j} \frac{Y}{(\log Y)^j}.$$ The implicit constant depends only on the shifts; the threshold for $Y$ may also depend on the finite exceptional range.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-shifted-sieve"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-shifted-sieve</summary>

*Proof.* Take $Q=\sqrt Y$ and remove from $C\cap[0,Y]$ all $c\le Q+C_0$, where $C_0$ is a fixed constant covering the exceptional range. The remaining set $U$ avoids $j$ distinct classes modulo every prime $p_0<p\le Q$, for a fixed sufficiently large $p_0$ depending on the shifts. We may assume $U\ne\varnothing$ and $p_0>j$.

At such a prime define a mean-zero function equal to $1$ on the $p-j$ allowed classes and to $-(p-j)/j$ on the forbidden classes. Its probability squared norm is $(p-j)/j$. For squarefree $u$ with prime factors in this range, the tensor product of these functions has mean $1$ under the projection of the uniform measure on $U$. Its additive Fourier expansion contains only primitive modes, since every local factor has mean zero. Parseval and Cauchy–Schwarz therefore give <a id="eq:prelim-primitive-lower"></a>$$\label{eq:prelim-primitive-lower}
 \sum_{\substack{h\bmod u\\(h,u)=1}}
 \left|\mathbb E_{n\in U}e_u(hn)\right|^2
 \ge \prod_{p\mid u}\frac{j}{p-j}.$$ The empty product at $u=1$ is included. Applying <a href="#eq:prelim-large-sieve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prelim-large-sieve">[eq:prelim-large-sieve]</a> with $c_n=\mathbf 1_U(n)/|U|$ bounds the sum of the left side over $u\le Q$ by $O(Y/|U|)$.

To bound the sum of the right side below, first allow every squarefree product of primes $p_0<p\le Q^{1/(10j)}$. Its total weight is $$\prod_{p_0<p\le Q^{1/(10j)}}\left(1+\frac{j}{p-j}\right)
 \gg_{b_1,\ldots,b_j} (\log Q)^j$$ by Mertens’ estimates. Under the probability law obtained by normalizing these weights, a prime is included with probability $j/p$, so $$\mathbb E\log u
 =j\sum_{p_0<p\le Q^{1/(10j)}}\frac{\log p}{p}
 =\left(\frac1{10}+o(1)\right)\log Q.$$ Markov’s inequality retains a fixed positive proportion of the weight when $u\le Q$. Thus $|U|\ll_{b_1,\ldots,b_j}Y/(\log Y)^j$. Restoring $O(\sqrt Y)$ removed points proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-factor"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-factor" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Finite summands). *If $A,B\subset\mathbb N_0$ have at least two elements each and $A+B$ agrees with $\mathcal P$ outside a finite set, then both $A$ and $B$ are infinite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-factor"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-factor</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $A$ is finite. Two distinct elements of $A$ give two shifts of $B$ to which Lemma <a href="#lem:shifted-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shifted-sieve">2</a> applies. Hence $B(Y)\ll Y/(\log Y)^2$. On the other hand, every sufficiently large prime at most $Y$ is represented using elements of $A\cap[0,Y]$ and $B\cap[0,Y]$, so $$\frac{Y}{\log Y}\ll A(Y)B(Y)
 \le |A|B(Y)\ll \frac{Y}{(\log Y)^2},$$ a contradiction. The other case is symmetric. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The two-infinite-set theorem

It remains to prove the following technical theorem.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (Two infinite summands). *There do not exist two infinite sets $A,B\subseteq\mathbb N_0$ such that $(A+B)\mathbin{\triangle}\mathcal P$ is finite.*

</div>

Together with Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-factor">3</a>, this proves Theorem <a href="#cor:ostmann" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:ostmann">1</a>: a counterexample to the latter would have both summands infinite and would therefore contradict Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">4</a>.

For the remainder of the proof, suppose to the contrary that infinite sets $A,B\subseteq\mathbb N_0$ satisfy this eventual equality. Fix a threshold $N$ so that both of the following statements hold: <a id="eq:eventual-equality"></a>$$\label{eq:eventual-equality}
 \mathcal P\cap(N,\infty)\subseteq A+B,
 \qquad (A+B)\cap(N,\infty)\subseteq\mathcal P.$$ The first is prime coverage; the second excludes composite sums. Choose an integer $N_*>|N|+10$ and put $D=-B$. Constants in $O(\cdot)$ and $\ll$ may depend on these fixed data; additional dependencies will be indicated where the order of parameters matters.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Complementary residue supports

For every prime $p$, the residue sets $$\{a\bmod p:a\in A,\ a>p+N_*\},\qquad
 \{d\bmod p:d\in D,\ -d>p+N_*\}$$ are disjoint. Indeed, a common residue would make $a-d$ a positive multiple of $p$ larger than $p$ and $N$, although $a-d\in A+B$ must be prime. Both sets are nonempty by infinitude. Choose a partition $S_p,S_p^c$ of $\mathbb F_p$ containing the respective sets, and write $\sigma_p=|S_p|/p$. Thus $0<\sigma_p<1$. Residues in neither tail support may be assigned to either part.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Sizes of the summands

The following bounds are due to Elsholtz (Elsholtz 2006, Theorem 1.9) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Elsholtz2006](#ref-Elsholtz2006)]. We include the large-sieve and collision argument in the present nonnegative-integer convention.

<a id="stmt-lem-size"></a>
<div id="lem:size" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:src1"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Square-root bounds). *Under the assumed decomposition with both summands infinite, $$\label{eq:src1}
 \frac{\sqrt Y}{(\log Y)^3}\ll A(Y),B(Y)
 \ll \sqrt Y(\log Y)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-size"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-size</summary>

*Proof.* Coverage and the prime number theorem give $A(Y)B(Y)\gg Y/\log Y$. Infinitude and Lemma <a href="#lem:shifted-sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shifted-sieve">2</a> give, for every fixed positive integer $j$, $$A(Y),B(Y)\ll_j\frac{Y}{(\log Y)^j},
 \qquad A(Y),B(Y)\gg_j(\log Y)^{j-1}.$$ The second assertion follows from the first and the product lower bound.

<a id="eq:prelim-H-lower"></a>
Suppose now that $m=A(x)\ge3\sqrt x$, with $x$ sufficiently large. The set $U=A\cap(\sqrt x+N_*,x]$ has size comparable to $m$. For $\sqrt x/2<p\le\sqrt x$ it avoids all the $\nu_p=|B\bmod p|$ classes in $-B\bmod p$. Here all elements of $B$ are allowed: if $p\mid a+b$, then $a+b>p,N$, contrary to primality. Parseval and Cauchy–Schwarz on the allowed classes give the nonzero-frequency energy lower bound $$\sum_{h\in\mathbb F_p^\times}|\mathbb E_{a\in U}e_p(ha)|^2
 \ge \frac{\nu_p}{p-\nu_p}\ge\frac{\nu_p}{p}.$$ The large sieve then implies $\sum_{\sqrt x/2<p\le\sqrt x}\nu_p/p\ll x/m$. Consequently Cauchy–Schwarz, followed by the prime number theorem, gives $$\label{eq:prelim-H-lower}
 H:=\sum_{\sqrt x/2<p\le\sqrt x}\frac{\log p}{\nu_p}
 \gg \frac{m}{\sqrt x\log x}.$$

<a id="eq:prelim-H-upper"></a>
For any $Y\ge2$ with $B(Y)>0$, consider two independent uniform elements of $B\cap[0,Y]$. Their collision probability modulo $p$ is at least $1/\nu_p$. Each unequal pair contributes at most $\log Y$ to the sum of $\log p$ over its prime divisors; equal pairs have probability $1/B(Y)$ and cost $O(\sqrt x)$. Hence $$\label{eq:prelim-H-upper}
 H\le\log Y+O\left(\frac{\sqrt x}{B(Y)}\right).$$ If $m>x^{1/2+\epsilon}$ on an unbounded sequence, for a fixed $\epsilon>0$, choose $\log Y=x^{\epsilon/2}$ in <a href="#eq:prelim-H-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prelim-H-upper">[eq:prelim-H-upper]</a>. Equation <a href="#eq:prelim-H-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prelim-H-lower">[eq:prelim-H-lower]</a> forces $B(Y)\ll\sqrt x$, whereas the preceding polylogarithmic lower bound contradicts this for sufficiently large fixed $j$. Applying the same argument to $B$, and using coverage, proves $A(x),B(x)=x^{1/2+o(1)}$.

We can now take $Y=x^2$ in <a href="#eq:prelim-H-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prelim-H-upper">[eq:prelim-H-upper]</a>, since $B(x^2)=x^{1+o(1)}$. Thus $H\ll\log x$, and <a href="#eq:prelim-H-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prelim-H-lower">[eq:prelim-H-lower]</a> yields $m\ll\sqrt x(\log x)^2$. If $m<3\sqrt x$ the same bound is immediate. The bound for $B$ is symmetric. Coverage once again gives both lower bounds in <a href="#eq:src1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src1">[eq:src1]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Collision stability

The congruent-pair count underlying Gallagher’s larger sieve (Gallagher 1971) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Gallagher1971](#ref-Gallagher1971)] also measures how close a summand is to uniform on its residue support. This quantitative use of the larger sieve is central to Green and Harper’s inverse-sieve arguments (Green and Harper 2014, Equation (2.1) and Lemma 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[GreenHarper](#ref-GreenHarper)]. We need the following weighted form for the two complementary supports.

Write $U_E$ for uniform probability on a finite nonempty set $E$. If $\mu$ is a probability measure on integers, write $\mu_p$ for its projection modulo $p$. The norms in the next statement are counting norms of probability mass functions.

<a id="stmt-lem-collision"></a>
<div id="lem:collision" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:src2"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Collision stability). *Let $b\ge1$ be fixed. Suppose $\mu,\nu$ are probability measures on $$A\cap(\sqrt X+N_*,X],\qquad
 D\cap[-X,-\sqrt X-N_*),$$ respectively, and that every point mass is at most $(\log X)^b/\sqrt X$. Put $Q=\sqrt X/(\log X)^{b+1}$. Then $$\label{eq:src2}
\begin{split}
 \sum_{p\le Q}\log p\bigg(
 &\|\mu_p-U_{S_p}\|_2^2+\|\nu_p-U_{S_p^c}\|_2^2\\
 &+\frac{\sigma_p^{-1}+(1-\sigma_p)^{-1}-4}{p}
 \bigg)\ll_b\log\log X.
\end{split}$$ All terms on the left are nonnegative.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-collision"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-collision</summary>

*Proof.* The same unequal-pair count used above gives $$\sum_{p\le Q}(\log p)\|\mu_p\|_2^2
 \le\log X+O\left(Q\max_n\mu(n)\right)
 =\log X+O(1),$$ and the corresponding bound holds for $\nu$. The projected supports lie in $S_p,S_p^c$. Therefore $$\|\mu_p-U_{S_p}\|_2^2=\|\mu_p\|_2^2-\frac1{|S_p|},
 \quad
 \|\nu_p-U_{S_p^c}\|_2^2=\|\nu_p\|_2^2-\frac1{|S_p^c|}.$$ The left side of <a href="#eq:src2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src2">[eq:src2]</a> is consequently the sum of the two collision energies minus $4\sum_{p\le Q}\log p/p$. Mertens’ estimate gives $$4\sum_{p\le Q}\frac{\log p}{p}
 =2\log X-4(b+1)\log\log X+O(1),$$ which proves the assertion. Nonnegativity follows also from $\sigma^{-1}+(1-\sigma)^{-1}\ge4$ for $0<\sigma<1$. ◻

</details>

One useful form of the lemma does not require pointwise bounded test functions. If $f_p:\mathbb F_p\to\mathbb C$ has probability squared norm at most one, then <a id="eq:prelim-test-stability"></a>$$\label{eq:prelim-test-stability}
 \left|\mathbb E_\mu f_p-\mathbb E_{S_p}f_p\right|^2
 \le p\|\mu_p-U_{S_p}\|_2^2,$$ by Cauchy–Schwarz. Thus these squared errors have total $\log p/p$-weighted sum $O_b(\log\log X)$; the same holds on the other side. In particular this applies to tests bounded by one. By Lemma <a href="#lem:size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:size">5</a>, uniform measures on $$A\cap[X^{9/10},X],\qquad D\cap[-X,-X^{9/10}]$$ meet the hypotheses for a fixed $b$: their sizes are $\gg\sqrt X/(\log X)^3$, while the discarded initial pieces have size $o(\sqrt X/(\log X)^3)$.

<a id="sec:quadratic"></a>
### Quadratic characters with arbitrary translating centres

We retain the sets $A,D=-B$, the partitions $S_p,S_p^c$, and the densities $\sigma_p=|S_p|/p$ from Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. For an odd prime $p$, let $\chi_p$ be the quadratic character of $\mathbb F_p$, extended by zero at zero. The translating residues in the following proposition need not arise from a common integer or rational number.

<a id="stmt-prop-quadratic"></a>
<div id="prop:quadratic" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:src3"></a>
**Proposition 7**. *Under the assumed eventual decomposition of the primes, $$\label{eq:src3}
 \sum_{T\leq \log p\leq 2T}\frac{\log p}{p}
 \max_{t\in\mathbb F_p}
 \left|\mathbb E_{x\in S_p}\chi_p(x-t)\right|=o(T)
 \qquad (T\longrightarrow\infty).$$*

</div>

The main difficulty is that the translating residues are initially unrelated. An amplified moment and Poisson summation, with estimates uniform over a polynomial-sized family of coefficient arrays, first produce a common rational centre for many primes. The quadratic large sieve then confines positive fractions of the two endpoint tails to a few quadratic kernels, whose populations contradict the collision estimate.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-quadratic"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-quadratic</summary>

*Proof.* **A biased prime block and the choice of scales.** Suppose that <a href="#eq:src3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src3">[eq:src3]</a> fails. Mertens’ estimate gives $$\sum_{T\leq\log p\leq2T}\frac{\log p}{p}=T+O(1).$$ Since each bias is at most one, there are a constant $\delta>0$ and an unbounded sequence of $T$ for which the primes with maximal bias at least $\delta$ have $\log p/p$-weight at least $c_\delta T$. Throughout this proof all limiting assertions refer to this sequence, and all constants may depend on this fixed bias. Set $$\rho=\frac35,\qquad \mu_0=10^{-6},\qquad \gamma=10^{-7},\qquad
 X=\exp(T^{1+\rho}),$$ and define $$A_X=A\cap[X^{9/10},X],\qquad
 D_X=D\cap[-X,-X^{9/10}].$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:size">5</a>, both tails have size $\gg \sqrt X/(\log X)^3$. Their uniform measures satisfy Lemma <a href="#lem:collision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:collision">6</a>, for example with its fixed exponent $b=4$. The prime bands used below lie below the corresponding cutoff $\sqrt X/(\log X)^5$.

<a id="eq:src4"></a>
The nonnegative density term in <a href="#eq:src2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src2">[eq:src2]</a> shows that primes with $\sigma_p\notin[1/3,2/3]$ have total $\log p/p$-weight $O(\log\log X)=O(\log T)$ in either of the bands considered here. For any functions $\phi_p:\mathbb F_p\to\mathbb C$ with $|\phi_p|\leq1$, the same equation and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\sum_{p\leq \sqrt X/(\log X)^5}\frac{\log p}{p}
 \left|\mathbb E_{x\in A_X}\phi_p(x)
       -\mathbb E_{x\in S_p}\phi_p(x)\right|^2
 \ll\log\log X,$$ and the analogous assertion for $D_X$ and $S_p^c$. This estimate allows the test function to be chosen separately at every prime. For each biased prime choose a maximizing translate and an orientation $\epsilon_p\in\{1,-1\}$. After deleting weight $O_\delta(\log T)$, both empirical means are within $\delta/8$ of the corresponding uniform means, and $1/3\leq\sigma_p\leq2/3$. The two uniform means are related by $$\mathbb E_{S_p^c}\chi_p(x-t)
   =-\frac{\sigma_p}{1-\sigma_p}\mathbb E_{S_p}\chi_p(x-t),$$ because the complete character sum is zero. Subdivision into intervals $[Z,2Z]$ therefore yields a set $\mathcal P\subset[Z,2Z]$ of primes, with $\log Z\asymp T$ and $J=|\mathcal P|\gg_\delta Z/\log Z$, such that $$\label{eq:src4}
 \mathbb E_{x\in A_X}\epsilon_p\chi_p(x-t_p)\geq c_\delta,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_{x\in D_X}\epsilon_p\chi_p(x-t_p)\leq-c_\delta
 \quad(p\in\mathcal P),$$ where $c_\delta>0$ is fixed. Indeed, the original band contains $O(T)$ such intervals, and one retains a positive constant of weighted mass in at least one interval; on it each prime has weight $O(\log Z/Z)$.

<a id="eq:quad-scales"></a>
Apply the density part of <a href="#eq:src2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src2">[eq:src2]</a> also to $T^\gamma\leq\log p\leq2T^\gamma$. Its full weight is $T^\gamma+O(1)$, while the exceptional weight is $O(\log T)$. Consequently some block $[z,2z]$, with $\log z\asymp T^\gamma$, contains $\gg z/\log z$ primes for which $1/3\leq\sigma_p\leq2/3$. Put $$K=\left\lfloor .02\frac{\log Z}{\log(2z)}\right\rfloor,$$ and let $L$ be a product of $K$ distinct such primes. There are enough primes to do this, since $z/\log z$ exceeds every fixed power of $T$. Let $k$ be the smallest even integer at least $\log X/\log Z+10$. Thus $$\label{eq:quad-scales}
 K\asymp T^{1-\gamma},\quad L\leq Z^{.02},\quad
 k\asymp T^\rho,\quad K=o(\log Z),\quad
 \frac Kk\gg T^{1-\gamma-\rho}.$$ In particular all factors of $L$ are distinct from the primes in $\mathcal P$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### An amplified moment and removal of repeated primes.

For $\ell\mid L$ prime write $f_\ell=\mathbf 1_{S_\ell}-\sigma_\ell$, periodically on $\mathbb Z$, and set $$\lambda=\frac1{16},\qquad
 W(n)=\prod_{\ell\mid L}(1+\lambda f_\ell(n)),\qquad
 H(n)=\frac1J\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\epsilon_p\chi_p(n-t_p).$$ Fix an even nonnegative Schwartz function $\psi$ that is bounded below by a positive constant on $[0,1]$ and has smooth compactly supported Fourier transform, with convention $\widehat\psi(y)=\int_\mathbb R\psi(x)e(-xy)\,dx$. Such a function is obtained by squaring the inverse Fourier transform of a sufficiently narrow real even smooth bump. The weight $W$ is positive, has period $L$ and mean one, and satisfies $W(n)\geq(1+\lambda/3)^K$ on $A_X$. Since $k$ is even, Jensen’s inequality and <a href="#eq:src4" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src4">[eq:src4]</a> give <a id="eq:quad-moment-lower"></a>$$\label{eq:quad-moment-lower}
 I:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}\psi(n/X)W(n)H(n)^k
   \gg \frac{\sqrt X}{(\log X)^3}(1+\lambda/3)^Kc_\delta^k
   \geq\sqrt X\exp(.016K)$$ for sufficiently large $T$. Here $\log(1+\lambda/3)>.0206$, and $k+\log\log X=o(K)$. Poisson summation, in its periodized form (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan 2007, Appendix D.2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan2007](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan2007)], gives for the $L$-periodic function $W$ <a id="eq:quad-total-weight"></a>$$\label{eq:quad-total-weight}
 I_0:=\sum_n\psi(n/X)W(n)=X\widehat\psi(0)\ll X:$$ all nonzero frequencies vanish once $X/L$ exceeds the fixed support radius of $\widehat\psi$.

<a id="eq:src5"></a>
We shall use the following elementary bound. If $H=J^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^J y_i$ with $y_i\in\{0,1,-1\}$ and $k$ is even, then, for an absolute constant $C$, $$\label{eq:src5}
 \left|H^k-\frac1{J^k}
       \sum_{\substack{i_1,\ldots,i_k\in[J]\\\text{distinct}}}
                   y_{i_1}\cdots y_{i_k}\right|
 \leq\sum_{1\leq j\leq k/2}
             \left(\frac{k^C}{J}\right)^j|H|^{k-2j}.$$ To prove it, express the distinct-index sum by Möbius inversion on set partitions; see (Rota 1964, sec. 7, Example 1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Rota1964](#ref-Rota1964)]. Equivalently, sum over permutations of $[k]$, with a cycle of length $m$ contributing $\sum_i y_i^m$ and with the usual permutation sign. A partition block of size $m$ has coefficient $(-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!$, since precisely $(m-1)!$ cycles have that support. If a permutation has $k-2j$ odd cycles, those cycles contribute $(JH)^{k-2j}$, whereas every even cycle contributes at most $J$ in absolute value. The total number of cycles is at most $k-j$. Moreover, at most $3j$ positions belong to cycles of length greater than one: an odd cycle of length $m\geq3$ uses $m$ positions while contributing $m-1$ to $2j$, and an even cycle contributes all its positions to $2j$. There are at most $k^{O(j)}$ permutations with this many moved positions. The identity permutation is the term $H^k$; all the other terms give <a href="#eq:src5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src5">[eq:src5]</a>.

Apply <a href="#eq:src5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src5">[eq:src5]</a> pointwise and then use Hölder with respect to the positive measure $\psi(n/X)W(n)$. The relative error in $I$ is at most $$\sum_{j=1}^{k/2}
 \left(\frac{k^C}{J}\left(\frac{I_0}{I}\right)^{2/k}\right)^j=o(1).$$ Indeed, the ratio is at most a constant times $k^C\log Z\exp(-.032K/k)$, because $X^{1/k}\leq Z$; <a href="#eq:quad-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quad-scales">[eq:quad-scales]</a> makes this tend to zero.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### Poisson summation with arbitrary translations.

<a id="eq:quad-R-range"></a>
<a id="eq:quad-g-properties"></a>
Henceforth choose an ordered tuple of $k$ distinct primes of $\mathcal P$ uniformly at random. Its product is denoted by $M$. Expectation with respect to these tuples will also be written $\mathbb E_M$, since the functions in use depend only on their product. Let $$\chi_M=\prod_{p\mid M}\chi_p,\qquad
 0\leq t_M<M,\quad t_M\equiv t_p\pmod p\quad(p\mid M),$$ and set $$R=M/X,\qquad \theta=t_M/M,\qquad
 h_0\equiv-t_M\overline M\pmod L,\quad 0\leq h_0<L.$$ The bars here and below indicate inverses modulo the modulus in the expression. The choice of $k$ gives, uniformly in the tuple, $$\label{eq:quad-R-range}
 Z^{10}\leq R\leq Z^{12+o(1)}.$$ For $d\mid L$ put $$f_d(x)=\prod_{\ell\mid d}f_\ell(x),\qquad f_1=1,\qquad
 g_d(b)=\sqrt d\,\mathbb E_{x\bmod d} f_d(x)e_d(-bx).$$ The product is interpreted by the Chinese remainder theorem. Fourier orthogonality and the same theorem imply $$\label{eq:quad-g-properties}
 \mathbb E_{b\bmod d}|g_d(b)|^2\leq1,\quad |g_d(b)|\leq\sqrt d,\quad
 g_d(b)=\prod_{\ell\mid d}
              g_\ell\bigl(b\overline{d/\ell}\bigr).$$ In particular $g_d$ vanishes on nonunits when $d>1$, since each $f_\ell$ has mean zero. We use $g_1=1$.

<a id="eq:src6"></a>
<a id="eq:quad-fourier-lower"></a>
Expand $W=\sum_{d\mid L}\lambda^{\omega(d)}f_d$. For a fixed $d$, Poisson summation modulo $Md$ gives the complete transform of $f_d(n)\chi_M(n-t_M)$. Its factor modulo $d$ is $\sqrt d\,g_d(-u\overline M)$, and its factor modulo $M$ is $$\tau(\chi_M)\chi_M(u)\chi_M(d)
       e_M(t_Mu\overline d),$$ where $|\tau(\chi_M)|=\sqrt M$ by the primitive quadratic Gauss-sum identity (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan 2007, Theorems 9.6–9.7) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan2007](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan2007)]. Since $t_M+Mh_0$ is divisible by every $d\mid L$, $$e_M(t_Mu\overline d)=e\bigl((\theta+h_0)u/d\bigr).$$ It follows that the transform of the distinct-prime contribution, after division by $\sqrt X$ and multiplication by a unit complex number depending only on the tuple, is $$\label{eq:src6}
 \sum_{d\mid L}\frac{\lambda^{\omega(d)}\chi_M(d)}{\sqrt{Rd}}
 \sum_{u\in\mathbb Z}\chi_M(u)g_d(-u\overline M)
       e\bigl((h_0+\theta)u/d\bigr)
       \widehat\psi\bigl(u/(Rd)\bigr).$$ The orientations $\prod_{p\mid M}\epsilon_p$ are included in that unit. The term $u=0$ vanishes. Since $f_d$ and $\psi$ are real, the negative-frequency part is $\chi_M(-1)$ times the conjugate of the positive-frequency part. Write the latter as $\mathcal F_M$. The normalization from sampling distinct tuples is $(J)_k/J^k=1+o(1)$, where $(a)_r=a(a-1)\cdots(a-r+1)$. The moment bound and the repeat estimate therefore imply $$\label{eq:quad-fourier-lower}
 \mathbb E_M|\mathcal F_M|\geq\exp(.015K).$$ For example, the absolute value of <a href="#eq:src6" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src6">[eq:src6]</a> is at most $2|\mathcal F_M|$, while the averaged original distinct contribution is $(1-o(1))I/\sqrt X$.

<a id="eq:src7"></a>
<a id="eq:src8"></a>
Write uniquely $u=svw^2>0$, where $s$ is squarefree and coprime to $L$, $v\mid L$, and $w\geq1$. For a positive integer $P$ define $$\label{eq:src7}
 \begin{aligned}
 N_{dsv}&=\sqrt{Rd/(sv)},\\
 G_d^{(P)}(s,v)&=\frac1{N_{dsv}}
   \sum_{\substack{w\geq1\\P\mid w}}
     g_d(-svw^2\overline M)
     e\bigl((h_0+\theta)svw^2/d\bigr)
     \widehat\psi\bigl(svw^2/(Rd)\bigr),\\
 C_s^{(P)}&=\sum_{v\mid L}\frac{\chi_M(v)}{\sqrt v}
            \sum_{d\mid L}\lambda^{\omega(d)}\chi_M(d)
                                G_d^{(P)}(s,v).
 \end{aligned}$$ Since $\chi_M(w^2)=\mathbf 1_{(w,M)=1}$, inclusion-exclusion gives $$\label{eq:src8}
 \mathcal F_M=
  \sum_{P\mid M}(-1)^{\omega(P)}
    \sum_{\substack{s\leq Z^{14}\\s\text{ squarefree}\\(s,L)=1}}
       \frac{\chi_M(s)}{\sqrt s}\,C_s^{(P)}.$$ The fixed support of $\widehat\psi$ and <a href="#eq:quad-R-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quad-R-range">[eq:quad-R-range]</a> ensure $u\ll RL<Z^{14}$ and $w<Z^7$. Consequently only $P\leq Z^7$ occur in this expression. A divisor of $M$ this small contains at most seven prime factors, so the number of outer terms is $O(k^7)$.

To relate the translating residues, we shall force a large value of $G_d^{(1)}(s,v)$ with $s<L^4$ for sufficiently many prime tuples. On a progression $w=x+dj$, its remaining quadratic phase has coefficient $svd\theta$, so a large value will give a small-denominator approximation to $\theta=t_M/M$. Since the arrays $C_s^{(P)}$ depend on the sampled tuple $M$, the intervening moment estimate must be uniform over a fixed family containing their discretizations.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### A moment estimate for a polynomial-sized family of arrays.

<a id="eq:src9"></a>
Put $l=\lfloor T^{\mu_0}\rfloor$ and $u_0=2l$. Let $\mathcal B$ be any fixed family of complex arrays $b=(b_s)$, indexed by the squarefrees $s\leq Z^{14}$ coprime to $L$. Suppose that, for a fixed constant $c$, $|\mathcal B|\leq Z^c$ and $\sum_s|b_s|\leq Z^c$ for every $b\in\mathcal B$. We claim $$\label{eq:src9}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\left(\mathbb E_M\max_{b\in\mathcal B}
           \left|\sum_s\chi_M(s)b_s\right|^{2l}\right)^{1/(2l)}
 \\
 &\qquad\leq \exp(o(K))\max_{b\in\mathcal B}
           \left(\sum_su_0^{\omega(s)}|b_s|^2\right)^{1/2}
       +O(Z^{-10}).
 \end{aligned}$$ The $o(K)$ is uniform for families with this fixed $c$.

To prove the claim, first replace the maximum of the moments by their sum. Each product $M$ has probability $k!/(J)_k\leq2k!/J^k$, so positivity permits domination by the sum over all odd $m\leq(2Z)^k$ with $\chi_M(s)$ replaced by $(s/m)$. Expand one $2l$-th moment. If the product $n$ of its $2l$ indices is nonsquare, $m\mapsto(n/m)$ on odd $m$, extended by zero on even $m$, is a nonprincipal quadratic Dirichlet character with possible additional zero factors. It has a period at most $8n$ and mean zero over a period. One can see this by using the nontrivial squarefree kernel of $n$ and then imposing coprimality to its square part; the induced character remains nonprincipal on the units of a modulus dividing $8\operatorname{rad}(n)$. As $n\leq Z^{28l}$, its sum over any initial interval is $O(Z^{28l})$. The total absolute nonsquare error for an array is therefore at most $Z^{28l}(\sum_s|b_s|)^{2l}=Z^{O_c(l)}$.

When the product of the indices is square, its Jacobi symbol is either zero or one. Its contribution in absolute value is bounded by $(2Z)^k$ times the $2l$-th moment of $$F(\varepsilon)=\sum_s|b_s|\prod_{p\mid s}\varepsilon_p,$$ where the $\varepsilon_p$ are independent uniform signs. Indeed, the expectation of a product of these monomials is one exactly when the product of the corresponding squarefree indices is a square, and is zero otherwise. We use the even-moment form of Bonami’s hypercontractive inequality (Bonami 1970, chap. III, Theorems 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Bonami1970](#ref-Bonami1970)] and include its elementary proof here. For real $x,y$ the binomial theorem gives $$\left(\mathbb E_{\varepsilon=\pm1}|x+\varepsilon y|^{2l}\right)^{1/l}
 \leq x^2+(2l-1)y^2,$$ because $\binom{2l}{2i}\leq\binom li(2l-1)^i$. Apply this one sign at a time. Minkowski’s inequality, applied to the square functions of the remaining signs, shows inductively that $$\|F\|_{2l}^2\leq
       \sum_s(2l-1)^{\omega(s)}|b_s|^2
 \leq\sum_su_0^{\omega(s)}|b_s|^2.$$ This is the tensorized sign-moment form of Bonami hypercontractivity; see (Bonami 1970, chap. III, Theorem 3, p. 376) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Bonami1970](#ref-Bonami1970)]. The preceding argument proves the needed form directly; the arithmetic moment transfer around it is the adaptation used here. Squarefreeness ensures that each sign occurs with degree at most one in every monomial.

The $2l$-th root of the square-product transfer factor is at most $$\left(Z^c\frac{2k!}{J^k}(2Z)^k\right)^{1/(2l)}
 \leq\exp\left(O_c\left(\frac{T+k\log T}{l}\right)\right)
 =\exp(o(K)).$$ The last assertion uses $\mu_0>\gamma$ and $\rho<1$. The nonsquare error, after the same probability factor and taking a $2l$-th root, is at most $$\exp\left(-\frac{k\log Z}{2l}
            +O_c(\log Z)+O\left(\frac{k\log T}{l}\right)\right),$$ which is $O(Z^{-10})$ since $l=o(k)$. This proves <a href="#eq:src9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src9">[eq:src9]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Discretizing the coefficients and removing $P\ne1$.

The coefficients in <a href="#eq:src7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src7">[eq:src7]</a>, except for their dependence on $\theta,R,P$, are determined by $M\bmod4L$ and $h_0\bmod L$. For the quadratic symbols of divisors of $L$, this follows from quadratic reciprocity; the inverse of $M$ modulo every $d\mid L$ is already determined modulo $L$. We allow every unit residue $M\bmod4L$, every $h_0\bmod L$, every integer $1\leq P\leq Z^7$, and grids of mesh at most $Z^{-100}$ for $$0\leq\theta\leq1,\qquad Z^{10}\leq R\leq2Z^{13}.$$ This is a family of $Z^{O(1)}$ parameter choices, with an absolute constant exponent. The larger range for $R$ also accommodates nearest grid points.

These grids approximate the coefficients uniformly. On the support of a summand in $G_d^{(P)}$ one has $w\ll N_{dsv}$ and $svw^2/d\ll R$. The number of positive multiples of $P$ in this range is $O(N_{dsv}/P)$, with no extra term required; if the range contains any multiple at all, then $N_{dsv}/P$ is bounded below. Differentiating the formula, and using the smooth compact support, gives uniformly $$|\partial_\theta G_d^{(P)}|\ll R\sqrt L/P,\qquad
 |\partial_R G_d^{(P)}|\ll \sqrt L/(RP).$$ The derivative with respect to $R$ includes the factor $N_{dsv}^{-1}$ and the argument of $\widehat\psi$; both have the indicated bound. Thus nearest grid replacement changes each $G_d^{(P)}(s,v)$ by $O(Z^{-80})$. The same estimates hold on the entire enlarged grid range, where the support still has $s<Z^{14}$ and $w<Z^7$. Even after summing the coefficient errors absolutely in <a href="#eq:src8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src8">[eq:src8]</a>, their contribution is $o(1)$: there are at most $Z^{14}$ indices, divisor sums cost $\exp(O(K))=Z^{o(1)}$, and there are $O(k^7)$ outer terms. The crude bound $|G_d^{(P)}|\ll\sqrt L/P$ also shows that every array $b_s=s^{-1/2}C_s^{(P)}$ has polynomial $\ell^1$ norm.

<a id="eq:src10"></a>
We shall repeatedly use $$\label{eq:src10}
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{v\mid L}v^{-1/2}&=1+o(1),\\
 \sum_{\substack{s\leq Z^{14}\\s\text{ squarefree}}}
       \frac{u_0^{\omega(s)}}s
 &\leq\prod_{p\leq Z^{14}}(1+u_0/p)\\
 &\leq\exp\left(u_0\sum_{p\leq Z^{14}}\frac1p\right)
 =\exp(o(K)).
 \end{aligned}$$ For the first assertion, the logarithm of the divisor product is $O(K/\sqrt z)=o(1)$. For the last one it is $O(T^{\mu_0}\log T)=o(K)$. If $P\mid M$ and $P\ne1$, then $P\geq Z$. For all arrays with this restriction, their weighted square norm in <a href="#eq:src9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src9">[eq:src9]</a> is at most $$Z^{-1}\sqrt L\,(1+\lambda)^K\exp(o(K))
   =Z^{-1+.01+o(1)}.$$ The maximum over all integer $P$ in the grid family bounds the adaptive choice of the actual divisors. Equation <a href="#eq:src9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src9">[eq:src9]</a> and the $O(k^7)$ count show that all $P\ne1$ terms in <a href="#eq:src8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src8">[eq:src8]</a> have total expected absolute value $o(1)$. We now set $P=1$ and suppress its superscript.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### A small-kernel quadratic sum must be large.

<a id="eq:src11"></a>
<a id="eq:quad-small-G-upper"></a>
<a id="eq:quad-small-off-event"></a>
We shall prove that with probability at least $\exp(-O(K))$ there exist a squarefree $s<L^4$ coprime to $L$ and divisors $v,d\mid L$ such that $$\label{eq:src11}
 |G_d(s,v)|\geq2^{\omega(d)/2}e^{-K}.$$ For $s<L^4$, on the entire grid range, $$\frac{N_{dsv}}d=\sqrt{\frac R{svd}}\geq Z^{4.9}$$ for sufficiently large $T$. If $sv$ is nonunit modulo $d$, the function $g_d(-svw^2\overline M)$ is identically zero. Otherwise, because $g_d$ vanishes at nonunits, the squaring map has fibers of size at most $2^{\omega(d)}$ on every relevant residue. Therefore $$\mathbb E_{w\bmod d}|g_d(-svw^2\overline M)|^2\leq2^{\omega(d)},
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_{w\bmod d}|g_d(-svw^2\overline M)|\leq2^{\omega(d)/2}.$$ The same assertions hold for $d=1$ by convention. In an interval of length $O(N_{dsv})$, each residue occurs $O(N_{dsv}/d)$ times. Consequently $$\label{eq:quad-small-G-upper}
 |G_d(s,v)|\ll2^{\omega(d)/2}\qquad(s<L^4).$$ By Minkowski and <a href="#eq:src10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src10">[eq:src10]</a>, the corresponding small-$s$ arrays satisfy $$\left(\sum_{s<L^4}\frac{u_0^{\omega(s)}}s|C_s|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \leq\exp(o(K))(1+\sqrt2\lambda)^K=\exp(O(K)).$$ If <a href="#eq:src11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src11">[eq:src11]</a> fails for an actual tuple, its nearest grid point belongs, for sufficiently large $T$, to the fixed subfamily on which every small-$s$ value is bounded by $2^{\omega(d)/2}\exp(-.9K)$. This follows since the mesh error $O(Z^{-80})$ is negligible compared with $e^{-K}$. The weighted square norm of each small-$s$ array in that subfamily is at most $$\label{eq:quad-small-off-event}
 \exp\bigl((- .9+\log(1+\sqrt2\lambda)+o(1))K\bigr),$$ which is exponentially small.

It remains to bound all large-$s$ arrays, uniformly in their parameters, by $\exp((.006+o(1))K)$ in the weighted square norm. Fix $v\mid L$ and a segment $S\leq s<2S$, where $L^4\leq S\leq Z^{14}$, and put $\tau=.005$. There are $O(\log Z)$ segments; their total cost in Minkowski’s inequality is $\exp(o(K))$, as is the sum over $v$ with the weights $v^{-1/2}$ in <a href="#eq:src10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src10">[eq:src10]</a>.

<a id="eq:src12"></a>
For the low-weight indices $u_0^{\omega(s)}\leq e^{\tau K}$, bound the squared norm by $e^{\tau K}$ times the unweighted one. By positivity this unweighted sum of squared moduli can be extended to all integers in $[S,2S)$ before expanding its $d,d'$ cross terms; the defining formula for $G_d(s,v)$ makes sense for these integers as well. We claim the correlation estimate $$\label{eq:src12}
 \left|\sum_{S\leq s<2S}
       \frac{G_d(s,v)\overline{G_{d'}(s,v)}}s\right|
 \ll\left(\frac{dd'}{(d,d')^2}\right)^{-1/2}.$$ To verify it, expand the two sums over $w,w'$. A nonzero summand has $$w\ll\sqrt{Rd/(Sv)},\qquad
 w'\ll\sqrt{Rd'/(Sv)}.$$ Its periodic factor is $F(s)=g_d(cs)\overline{g_{d'}(c's)}$, where $c=-vw^2\overline M\pmod d$ and $c'=-v(w')^2\overline M\pmod{d'}$. If either scalar is a nonunit, the summand is identically zero. Otherwise, on the period $q=[d,d']$, every normalized additive Fourier coefficient of $F$ is bounded in modulus by $$A_{d,d'}=\left(\frac{dd'}{(d,d')^2}\right)^{-1/2}.$$ Indeed, at a prime in exactly one divisor, Fourier inversion of $g_\ell$ gives an upper bound $1/\sqrt\ell$ since $|f_\ell|\leq1$. At a common prime Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:quad-g-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quad-g-properties">[eq:quad-g-properties]</a> give an upper bound one. Chinese remaindering multiplies these local bounds.

<a id="eq:quad-low-weight"></a>
The remaining phase is $e(\alpha s)$ for an arbitrary real number $\alpha$, and the two transform factors form a smooth weight with uniformly bounded supremum and total variation on $[S,2S]$. For a $q$-periodic function whose Fourier coefficients are at most $A_{d,d'}$, finite Fourier expansion and the geometric-sum bound give $$\left|\sum_{s\in I}F(s)e(\alpha s)\right|
 \ll A_{d,d'}\bigl(|I|+q\log(2q)\bigr)$$ for an interval $I$ of length at most $S$.
