<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### The exact transfer and its diagonal

<a id="eq:src53"></a>
We verify the relation between the successive expressions <a href="#eq:src49" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src49">[eq:src49]</a>. Fix $\mathbf d,p,\mathbf u$ at step $j$, put $P=pu$, and group old terms according to $t=v/H\pmod P$. On the original prime support, $$G_{PH}(v/d)=G_P(t/d)G_H(v/(Pd)).$$ Let $$B_t=\mathbb E_H
       \sum_{\substack{0<|v|\le V_{j-1}\\v/H\equiv t\ ({\rm mod}\ P)}}
                G_H(v/(Pd))A_{j-1}(v),$$ with all inherited support conditions; invalid terms are zero. The extracted row has counting squared norm at most $P$: $$\sum_{t\bmod P}|G_P(t/d)|^2\le P.$$ This is CRT and the exact probability squared norm one at each compensation prime, together with squared norm at most one at the giant. Cauchy–Schwarz in this row, followed by Jensen over the outer probability measures, yields $$|\eta_{j-1}|^2
                    \le\mathbb E_{\mathbf d,p,\mathbf u}
                                  P\sum_{t\bmod P}|B_t|^2.$$ Crucially, $B_t$ contains no prime-specific transform of the pivot giant $p$. It is defined for a positive integer $p$ by exactly the same formulas, with the same coprimality and frequency filters. Equation <a href="#eq:construction-giant-prior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:construction-giant-prior">[eq:construction-giant-prior]</a> gives $p\mu_G(p)=e^{c_g}\varphi(\log p-G)$ at primes. Extending the nonnegative sum to all positive integers gives $$|\eta_{j-1}|^2
   \le e^{c_g}\mathbb E_{\mathbf d,\mathbf u}
          \sum_{p\in\mathbb N}u\varphi(\log p-G)
                         \sum_{t\bmod pu}|B_t|^2.
 \label{eq:src53}$$ Here the sum over $p\in\mathbb N$ means positive integers.

Expand the square using independent $H_+,H_-$ and child frequencies $v,w$. Separate the terms $vH_-=wH_+$ as the diagonal. For every other term, the congruence of the two group indices defines the nonzero signed integer $s=(vH_--wH_+)/(pu)$, so the substitution is exactly <a href="#eq:src51" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src51">[eq:src51]</a>. It is one-to-one, with $p$ still any positive integer in its cell. Equations <a href="#eq:src43" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src43">[eq:src43]</a> and <a href="#eq:src48" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src48">[eq:src48]</a> show $$\log|s|\le E_{j-1}+\Delta_j+O_k(1)<E_j.$$ The margin is $2^{j-1}\sqrt m$, which tends to infinity. All protected bins used in this size estimate are present in each nonzero child history.

The actual lists $H_+,H_-$ cannot share a prime on such a term. If $\ell$ divided both products, it would divide $s$, since it is coprime to $pu$; but $\ell>|s|$. At a prime of $H_+$ and a prime of $H_-$, respectively, <a href="#eq:src51" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src51">[eq:src51]</a> gives $$v/P=s/H_-,\qquad -w/P=s/H_+.$$ The inverse tests are real, so $g_\ell^{\rm reg}(-b)=\overline{g_\ell^{\rm reg}(b)}$. The two regular transforms therefore combine exactly into $G_{H_+H_-}(s/d)$. The remaining weights, histories, and inserted pivot are precisely the recursive definition of $A_j$. Additional giant-frequency tests at the new top are free because its two giants are actual primes. Consequently the off-diagonal part of the extended expression in <a href="#eq:src53" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src53">[eq:src53]</a>, before $e^{c_g}$, is exactly $\eta_j$.

Write $\mathcal D_j$ for its exact diagonal. It is nonnegative, as we now verify, and we have <a id="eq:construction-transfer-split"></a>$$e^{-c_g}|\eta_{j-1}|^2
                         \le\mathcal D_j+\eta_j.
 \label{eq:construction-transfer-split}$$ In particular the off-diagonal expression $\eta_j$ is real: the extended square and its diagonal are real.

<a id="eq:src54"></a>
On the diagonal, all prime factors of $H_+$ and $H_-$ exceed the frequencies, so $vH_-=wH_+$ forces $H_+=H_-$ as products and $v=w$. Grouping by this common product and frequency *before* expanding the square gives a common factor $|G_H(v/(pud))|^2$ times the squared absolute value of the prior-weighted sum of coefficients over its ordered arrangements. This proves nonnegativity. The common factor is independent of the ordering: each prime uses the same local transform in every arrangement, and the single giant in $H$ is fixed by its disjoint range. We may therefore drop its bounded giant factor in this nonnegative expression. The remaining multiplier is $$\mathcal G=\prod_{\ell\mid H_{\rm sm}}
       \left|g_\ell\bigl(v/(pud(H/\ell))\bmod\ell\bigr)\right|^2,
 \label{eq:src54}$$ where $H_{\rm sm}$ consists of the small slots of $H$.

Now expand by bijections matching the slots in the two $H$ lists. This counts every ordered counterpart once, since the prime values within a valid $H$ are distinct. Only matches within the same prime band can occur. In particular the giant matches itself and bulk positions match bulk positions.

<a id="eq:construction-external-measure"></a>
Fix a first ordered $H$. For any prescribed counterpart ordering, its point probability is $C_H/H$ times its smooth cell factors and its role-membership indicators. The normalizing constants satisfy <a id="eq:construction-counterpart-mass"></a>$$C_H\le L^{-r_jm}\exp(Cr_jm+O_k(1)).
 \label{eq:construction-counterpart-mass}$$ Indeed there are $r_jm$ bulk positions, and $O(kr_j)$ cell positions. The latter normalizations cost $\exp(O(kr_jL)+O_k(1))$, absorbed uniformly by $\exp(Cr_jm)$ because $m\asymp k^4L$. On simultaneous coefficient support, <a href="#eq:src48" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src48">[eq:src48]</a> allows us to extract $C_He^{-T_H}$, leaving $e^{T_H}/H=O_k(1)$. Normalize the external integer measure as $$\int f(p)\,d\mu_{\mathrm{ext}}(p)
          :=e^{-G}\sum_{p\ge1}\varphi(\log p-G)f(p).
 \label{eq:construction-external-measure}$$ It has bounded total mass. Extracting $e^G$ from its definition and $e^{r_jw_j}$ from $u$ leaves the net scalar $C_He^{-\Delta_j}$. The remaining Archimedean factor is $$K_{\rm ar}
   =\frac{e^{T_H}}H\frac{u}{e^{r_jw_j}}
        \prod_{\text{counterpart cell positions}}
                           \varphi(\log p_i-c_i).$$ This factor is $O_k(1)$ on simultaneous coefficient support. Counterpart role-membership indicators are retained as zero conventions in addition to these displayed smooth factors.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### The two comparison estimates

We state precisely the comparison estimates needed for the diagonal and the final symmetrization. Their proof occupies Section <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">8</a>. At level $l$, put $r=2^l$. Each coefficient $A_l$ has $r$ bottom leaves, each carrying the $m$ bulk positions of the initial full list. Its $m$ outside spectator positions are shared by all leaves.

There are two outer environments, always using the priors, integer extension, coefficients, and support conventions just defined.

1.  In the *final environment*, $l=k$, the current list is $\mathcal I_k$ with its actual prime priors. Set $\mathcal G=1$. A pair of assignments may permute all $rm$ bulk values among their labeled positions; the nonbulk positions and the outside spectator list are common. Set $K_{\rm ar}=1$.

2.  In the *diagonal environment* for step $j$, $l=j-1$. The current $+$ giant $p$ has the external measure <a href="#eq:construction-external-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:construction-external-measure">[eq:construction-external-measure]</a>. All other current slots, including the type-$j$ vector $\mathbf u$, have their original actual prime priors. Set $\mathcal G$ equal to <a href="#eq:src54" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src54">[eq:src54]</a> with $v=s$, with the zero convention on invalid top support. In a pair of assignments the first uses the ordered $H$ and the second uses a prescribed same-band matching of its slots, with $p,\mathbf u,
           \mathbf d$ unchanged. Use the factor $K_{\rm ar}$ above, including the counterpart role indicators. For the norm of a *single* assignment this factor is omitted.

The expectation in the second environment includes the bounded-mass integer measure; it need not be a probability measure. All root-frequency sums below are over $0<|s|\le V_l$.

For a pair of assignments, form the bipartite multigraph whose two vertex sets are their respective $r$ bottom leaves. Each actual bulk variable is an edge joining the leaf that contains it in the first assignment to the leaf that contains it in the second. Every vertex has degree $m$. The graph and its number of connected components depend only on the slot assignments, not on the sampled prime values or on internal histories.

<a id="stmt-prop-comparisons"></a>
<div id="prop:comparisons" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:src55"></a>
<a id="eq:src56"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Comparison estimates). *In either of the two outer environments, the following estimates hold uniformly over the indicated assignments. For one assignment, $$\mathbb E\sum_{0<|s|\le V_l}\mathcal G|A_l(s)|^2
           \le\exp\bigl(r(\Delta_0+Cm)+o_k(m)\bigr).
 \label{eq:src55}$$ The constant $C$ is independent of sufficiently large $k$ and of the fixed gap budgets. For a pair of assignments, if $l\ge2$ and its overlap graph has at most $3r/4$ connected components, then $$\left|\mathbb E\sum_{0<|s|\le V_l}\mathcal G K_{\rm ar}
            A_l^{(1)}(s)\overline{A_l^{(2)}(s)}\right|
                        \le\exp(-\omega_k(m)).
 \label{eq:src56}$$ Here $\omega_k(m)/m\to\infty$ as $L\to\infty$ for every fixed choice of the other parameters. Equivalently, the right side of <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a> is smaller than $\exp(-C_km)$ for every fixed $C_k$ once $L$ is sufficiently large. The estimate is uniform in the allowed slot matchings and permutations.*

</div>

The assumptions about all current-state coprimalities and all giant-frequency unit tests are part of this proposition. They are used in its arithmetic reduction, not additional conclusions of the estimate. The outside spectator list is not resampled within either coefficient or between a paired comparison. In contrast, the two coefficients have independent internal prime samples; within each coefficient, a sample at one node is shared by its two children as specified in <a href="#eq:src52" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src52">[eq:src52]</a>. The assigned actual top values have the stated coupling between the coefficients. Coincidences among independently sampled internal prime values are allowed when the support permits them, and their equality patterns will be summed explicitly in the proof.

<a id="sec:arithmetic"></a>
### Arithmetic comparison of the histories

We prove Proposition <a href="#prop:comparisons" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparisons">16</a>. Throughout this section all the constants in the construction and the depth $k$ are fixed, and then $L$ tends to infinity. The notation, priors, templates, and support conventions are those of Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">7</a>. At comparison level $l$ put $r=2^l$. We treat both the final environment and the diagonal environment of Proposition <a href="#prop:comparisons" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparisons">16</a>; in the latter the positive giant is the external integer variable. All estimates below are uniform in the assignments being compared and in the permitted frequency histories.

The arithmetic reduction first integrates the top giants at the regular and internal small primes. After resolving the resulting internal-prime conditions, we replace the bulk primes by real log coordinates and independent unit residues, keeping the real weights and frequency tests. The paired estimate <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a> then uses signed spectator correlations. For the norm estimate <a href="#eq:src55" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src55">[eq:src55]</a>, we may take absolute values termwise, but must retain frequency divisibility to control the sum over histories.

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Histories, supports, and real-variable weights

Expand the two amplitudes in a comparison into their frequency histories. For the norm estimate <a href="#eq:src55" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src55">[eq:src55]</a>, use the same assignment in the two histories. At level $t\geq1$ there are $2^{l-t}$ nodes in a history, and its sampled compensation vector has $4\cdot2^{t-1}$ entries. Thus the total number of internal prime samples in the two histories is $O(kr)$. This counts each sample when it is made, rather than each subsequent occurrence of that sample in a descendant list.

Partition these samples according to their equality pattern. There are $O_k(1)$ patterns. Entries in a single compensation vector must be distinct, and the disjoint size ranges imply that equality is possible only between samples of the same compensation type. For each distinct internal sampled prime $b$, let $n_b$ be its multiplicity in the pattern. The product of the weights in <a href="#eq:src52" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src52">[eq:src52]</a> is then $$\prod_b b^{n_b}.$$ We retain all the other support restrictions for the moment.

<a id="eq:arith-frequency-modulus"></a>
Write $x_+,x_-$ for the top giant entries. The negative giant $x_-$ is an actual prime in both environments; $x_+$ is a prime in the final environment and an external positive integer in the diagonal environment. Let $d$ be the product of the $m$ distinct spectator primes, and let $c_{\rm sm}$ be the product of the distinct actual regular small primes at the top. The internal samples are disjoint from these primes by their types. Define $$\label{eq:arith-frequency-modulus}
 R_f=\prod_{\nu}|\nu|,\qquad Q_f=R_f^{k+2},
 \qquad \log Q_f=O_k(m),$$ where the product includes every frequency in the two histories. Repetitions in this product are allowed. A modulus equal to one has its usual trivial interpretation. Every small prime in the construction is larger than every frequency and hence is coprime to $Q_f$.

<a id="eq:arith-child-products"></a>
At a reversing node write the two current giant entries as $X_+,X_-$ and factor its child products as $$\label{eq:arith-child-products}
 H_+=X_+C_+M_+,\qquad H_-=X_-C_-M_-.$$ Here $M_\pm$ are the products of the bulk slots in the respective child subtrees, and $C_\pm$ contain their other regular small factors. The latter may include compensation samples from preceding reversals on the path. Both current giants are rational linear functions of $x_+,x_-$, by <a href="#eq:src51" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src51">[eq:src51]</a>.

<a id="eq:arith-numerator"></a>
We first justify an exhaustive residue description of the support. Assume temporarily that the top giants are coprime and that both are units modulo $c_{\rm sm}d$. Suppose that a current state is pairwise coprime. At its reversal the numerator is $$\label{eq:arith-numerator}
             N=vH_--wH_+,\qquad p=\frac{N}{su}.$$ Every prime divisor of $H_+$ is coprime to $H_-$ and to $v$: for an actual small prime this follows by size, and for a giant factor it is one of the giant–frequency unit conditions. Hence $N$ is a unit at every prime factor of $H_+$. The same argument, interchanging the two sides, applies to $H_-$. If $p$ is integral, it follows from $N=sup$ that both $p$ and $u$ are automatically coprime to every inherited slot in $H_+H_-$. In particular, a new compensation sample cannot divide an inherited giant. To obtain a pairwise-coprime child state, it remains only to require that $p$ be a unit against its own $u$ and against $d$.

The following tests therefore describe all the remaining arithmetic support:

1.  Modulo frequency factors, impose $s\mid N$ at every node and impose all giant–frequency unit conditions. These tests are determined by the top and small coordinates modulo $Q_f$. Division by a node frequency loses at most one factor $R_f$ of precision; the $u$’s are units modulo $Q_f$. At most $k$ successive divisions occur, so <a href="#eq:arith-frequency-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-frequency-modulus">[eq:arith-frequency-modulus]</a> leaves enough precision for every subsequent test.

<a id="eq:arith-internal-tests"></a>
2.  For each $b\mid u$ at a node, require $$\label{eq:arith-internal-tests}
                            N\equiv0\pmod b,
                 \qquad N\not\equiv0\pmod{b^2}.$$ The entries of the current $u$ are distinct and are coprime to $s$, so these are exactly its remaining integrality and own-$u$ unit requirements. Ancestor denominators involve other compensation types and frequencies; they are units even modulo $b^2$. Thus the tests can also be computed directly from the rational expressions in the top giants.

3.  Require the top giant units modulo $c_{\rm sm}d$. At each spectator $q\mid d$, use the zero convention in <a href="#eq:src36" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src36">[eq:src36]</a> whenever an inserted giant vanishes modulo $q$.

These tests, together with positivity and the real cutoffs, agree with ordinary recursive evaluation over the integers. Indeed, assuming the ancestors have already been evaluated integrally, the first two tests give divisibility by the relatively prime factors $s$ and $u$. The preceding coprimality argument then passes the required support to the children. This proves the assertion by induction down the tree.

<a id="eq:arith-spectator-product"></a>
At a spectator $q$, put $D'=d/q$. Formula <a href="#eq:arith-child-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-child-products">[eq:arith-child-products]</a>, the order of the child giants, and <a href="#eq:src51" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src51">[eq:src51]</a> identify the spectator factors from a history with the tree value in <a href="#eq:src36" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src36">[eq:src36]</a>. In particular, if the bulk product at leaf $i$ is $M_i$, the constants at a child have the product consistency required in that formula. Denote the two tree values by $W_{1,q},W_{2,q}$. Their combined spectator factor is $$\label{eq:arith-spectator-product}
                         \prod_{q\mid d}W_{1,q}\overline{W_{2,q}}.$$ The zero convention incorporates precisely the inserted-giant unit tests at $q$.

Let $\Xi$ denote the product of all smooth real factors in the two histories, including $K_{\rm ar}$ when it is present, but excluding $\prod_b b^{n_b}$. Define it for real positive top and small variables by using <a href="#eq:src51" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src51">[eq:src51]</a> over $\mathbb R$. Set the contribution to zero if an inserted $p$ is nonpositive. This is a smooth extension: the factor $\varphi(\log p-G)$ already vanishes for $p<e^{G-1}$. Counterpart role indicators involving nonbulk slots are retained as restrictions on those variables; all bulk priors and their role ranges are identical, so no such indicator cuts a bulk log coordinate.

<a id="eq:arith-xi-bound"></a>
There are $r$ leaf factors in each history. On their common support, the modulus in each instance of <a href="#eq:src50" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src50">[eq:src50]</a> has logarithm $\log X+\Delta_0+O_k(1)$. This follows from the giant cutoffs, all the specified small-prime ranges, and both sum bins at that leaf. Consequently $$\label{eq:arith-xi-bound}
                         |\Xi|\leq e^{-r\Delta_0+O_k(1)}.$$ Its first derivatives in the log coordinates of the top giants and bulk slots are bounded by $\exp(O_k(m))$. To check the possible cancellation in <a href="#eq:arith-numerator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-numerator">[eq:arith-numerator]</a>, use <a href="#eq:src48" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src48">[eq:src48]</a> at each subtree. Each of $|vH_-|$ and $|wH_+|$, divided by $|s|up$, is at most $$\exp(\Delta_j+E_{j-1}+O_k(1))$$ at a node of level $j$. The depth is bounded by $k$, so repeated logarithmic differentiation along a path costs $\exp(O_k(m))$. The remaining cutoffs, the bounded Fourier arguments in the leaf factors, and $K_{\rm ar}$ satisfy the same bound. The bounds extend over support boundaries: any problematic nonpositive inserted value lies outside the giant cutoff, where the smooth extension is zero. We use the actual prior ranges as integration domains, so no extra discontinuous real indicator is needed.

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### A progression estimate retaining the exceptional term

<a id="eq:arith-progression-scales"></a>
We record the precise approximation needed for the two successive idealizations. In the following table, either row may be used: $$\label{eq:arith-progression-scales}
\begin{array}{c|rrrrrr}
 &a_0&a_1&\mu&\delta&\delta'&\theta\\ \hline
 \mathrm{giant}&.049&.95&.012&.015&.018&.022\\
 \mathrm{bulk}&.0039&.007&.0012&.0014&.0016&.0018
\end{array}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-arith-progression"></a>
<div id="lem:arith-progression" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:src57"></a>
**Lemma 17**. *Fix a row of <a href="#eq:arith-progression-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-progression-scales">[eq:arith-progression-scales]</a>. There is at most one primitive real character $\chi_*$, with conductor at most $\exp(\exp(\mu L))$, and one associated real zero $\beta_*<1$ which must be retained in the following formula. If $$\log M_*\leq e^{\mu L},\qquad (a,M_*)=1,$$ and $I$ is an interval of length at most one in $[e^{a_0L},e^{a_1L}]$, then $$\label{eq:src57}
 \sum_{\substack{p\ \text{prime}\,,\ \log p\in I\\p\equiv a\pmod{M_*}}}
       \frac1p
 =\frac1{\phi(M_*)}\int_I
       \bigl(1-\chi_*(a)e^{(\beta_*-1)t}\bigr)\frac{dt}{t}
   +O\bigl(\exp(-c e^{\delta'L})\bigr).$$ The exceptional term is omitted if no such character exists or its conductor does not divide $M_*$. The implied constant is absolute for the fixed row. The multiplier inside the integral lies in $[0,2]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-arith-progression"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-arith-progression</summary>

*Proof.* Put $Q=\exp(e^{\mu L})$ and $S=\exp(e^{\theta L})$. The classical zero-free region and Page’s theorem give, simultaneously for primitive conductors up to $Q$ and ordinates of absolute value at most $S$, <a id="eq:arith-zero-free"></a>$$\label{eq:arith-zero-free}
                     1-\Re\rho\geq c_0e^{-\theta L},$$ apart from at most one simple real zero of a primitive real character. One may take for $\chi_*$ the character of a zero in this latter narrow region, if there is one. This follows from (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan 2007, Theorem 11.3 and Corollary 11.10) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan2007](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan2007)]; the analogous zero-free statement for $\zeta$ has no exception. We also use the standard zero count $N(T,\chi)\ll T\log(q(T+2))$ for primitive conductor $q$; see (Helfgott 2019, sec. 3.7.3) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Helfgott](#ref-Helfgott)].

<a id="eq:arith-smoothed-explicit"></a>
Here are details giving the required short-interval precision. Let $t_0$ be the left endpoint of $I$, put $y=e^{t_0}$, and set $\epsilon=\exp(-e^{\delta'L})$. Sandwich the indicator of the corresponding interval in the variable $u=n/y$ between smooth nonnegative functions $f_-,f_+$ supported in $[1/2,4]$. They may be chosen with $$\int(f_+-f_-)\,du=O(\epsilon),\qquad
 \|f_\pm^{(j)}\|_\infty\ll_j\epsilon^{-j}.$$ This remains possible when the interval is shorter than $\epsilon$, by taking the lower function zero. For either smooth function let $$F(u)=\frac{f(u)}{u\log(yu)},\qquad
 \mathcal M F(s)=\int_0^\infty F(u)u^{s-1}\,du.$$ For a primitive character $\chi$, Mellin inversion and a contour shift give $$\label{eq:arith-smoothed-explicit}
 \frac1y\sum_{n\geq1}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)F(n/y)
 =\mathbf 1_{\chi=1}\int_0^\infty F(u)\,du
   -\sum_\rho y^{\rho-1}\mathcal M F(\rho)
   +O\bigl(y^{-1+o(1)}\bigr).$$ The sum is over nontrivial zeros. For completeness, shift the Mellin integral of $-L'/L$ to real part $-1/2$, taking limits through heights avoiding zeros. The functional equation bounds the logarithmic derivative on that line by $O(\log(q(|t|+2)))$. Mellin decay makes the shifted integral convergent and bounded by a fixed power of $\epsilon^{-1}$ times $y^{-3/2}\log(q+2)$; a possible residue at zero costs $y^{-1}$ times such a factor. Because $a_0>\delta'$, these bounds have the stated size. The pole and zero residues are exactly those displayed in <a href="#eq:arith-smoothed-explicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-smoothed-explicit">[eq:arith-smoothed-explicit]</a>. This is the usual explicit-formula argument underlying (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan 2007, Theorem 11.16) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan2007](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan2007)].

<a id="eq:arith-mellin-decay"></a>
<a id="eq:arith-low-zero-error"></a>
On $0\leq\Re s\leq1$, integration by parts gives, for fixed $j$, $$\label{eq:arith-mellin-decay}
 |\mathcal M F(s)|\ll1,\qquad
 |\mathcal M F(s)|\ll_j
       \epsilon^{-O(j)}(1+|\Im s|)^{-j}.$$ The nonexceptional zeros of height at most $S$ therefore contribute at most $$\label{eq:arith-low-zero-error}
 \exp\bigl(-c e^{(a_0-\theta)L}+e^{\theta L}+O(L)\bigr).$$ For the giant row $a_0-\theta=.027>.022=\theta>\delta'$, and for the bulk row $a_0-\theta=.0021>.0018=\theta>\delta'$. Thus <a href="#eq:arith-low-zero-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-low-zero-error">[eq:arith-low-zero-error]</a> is smaller than the required error. For zeros above $S$, dyadic summation of the zero count and <a href="#eq:arith-mellin-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-mellin-decay">[eq:arith-mellin-decay]</a> gives $$\exp\bigl(C_j e^{\delta'L}-(j-1)e^{\theta L}+O(L)\bigr),$$ which is also sufficient, since $\delta'<\theta$.

For a prime $n=p$, the summand $\Lambda(p)F(p/y)/y$ is $f(p/y)/p$. Prime powers of higher exponent give $y^{-1/2+o(1)}$. Passing from an induced character modulo $M_*$ to its primitive character changes only the prime powers at prime divisors of $M_*$ and gives a negligible error as well. Orthogonality of the characters modulo $M_*$ now selects the residue class $a$; its factor $1/\phi(M_*)$ cancels the number of character errors. The possible exceptional primitive character occurs among these induced characters precisely when its conductor divides $M_*$.

Under $u=e^{t-t_0}$, the principal integral becomes $f(e^{t-t_0})\,dt/t$. The exceptional zero term becomes $$f(e^{t-t_0})e^{(\beta_*-1)t}\frac{dt}{t}.$$ In particular no factor $\beta_*$ is left over. Equivalently, this follows by differentiating the exceptional Chebyshev term $-\chi_*(a)y^{\beta_*}/(\beta_*\phi(M_*))$; see (Hugh L. Montgomery and Vaughan 2007, Corollaries 11.17 and 11.20) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[MontgomeryVaughan2007](#ref-MontgomeryVaughan2007)]. The resulting density is nonnegative and at most $2\,dt/(\phi(M_*)t)$. The upper and lower smooth tests therefore differ in their main integrals by $O(\epsilon)$, and their pointwise sandwich on the positive prime measure proves <a href="#eq:src57" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src57">[eq:src57]</a>. ◻

</details>

For each row, choose the possible exceptional character in Lemma <a href="#lem:arith-progression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arith-progression">17</a> before fixing the nongiant priors. If its conductor has a prime factor at least <a id="eq:arith-deletion-threshold"></a>$$\label{eq:arith-deletion-threshold}
                         T_*=\exp(e^{.0004L}),$$ delete one such prime value from every nongiant prior. This prescribes at most two deletions, as allowed in the construction. Every prime factor of the moduli below outside $Q_f$ exceeds $T_*$, whereas every prime factor of $Q_f$ is smaller than $T_*$ by <a href="#eq:arith-frequency-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-frequency-modulus">[eq:arith-frequency-modulus]</a>. Hence if an exceptional conductor still divides one of these moduli, its full conductor divides $Q_f$. Indeed, a selected large prime factor has been excluded, and otherwise all conductor prime factors must lie in the $Q_f$ part; coprimality with the other parts also accounts for their exponents. Thus every surviving Page correction depends only on the real coordinate and the $Q_f$ residue. It does not couple any of the other CRT coordinates.

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
#### Idealization of the top giants

<a id="eq:arith-small-size-budget"></a>
Condition on all actual small primes, including the internal samples in both histories. At fixed frequencies, the logarithm of the product of their large weights and all pointwise transform bounds is at most $e^{.012L}$ for large $L$. More explicitly, the regular and internal small-prime products have logarithms $$\label{eq:arith-small-size-budget}
 O_k(e^{.01L})+O_k(m e^{.006L}),$$ and the spectator products have still smaller logarithms. Use $|g_\ell|\leq\sqrt\ell$ and $|\widetilde g_\ell|\leq1$ for their local pointwise bounds. All the factors $\prod b^{n_b}$ are included in this estimate.

We may now remove the temporary assumption that the two top giants are coprime, using the residue description just obtained as the extension. In the prime environment the discrepancy is equality of the two primes; its probability is at most $e^{-G+O(L)}$. In the external-integer environment, a fixed prime $x_-$ has only $O(1)$ positive multiples in the log cell for $x_+$, and their normalized total mass is $O(e^{-G})$. Multiplying by the preceding pointwise bound is harmless, because $G\geq e^{.049L}$.

Use the modulus <a id="eq:arith-giant-modulus"></a>$$\label{eq:arith-giant-modulus}
                         M_*=c_{\rm sm}dQ_f\prod_b b^2.$$ Its factors are pairwise coprime, and <a href="#eq:arith-small-size-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-small-size-budget">[eq:arith-small-size-budget]</a> gives $\log M_*\leq e^{.012L}$. The giant log cells lie in $[e^{.049L},e^{.95L}]$, by their selection from <a href="#eq:src28" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src28">[eq:src28]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:arith-progression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arith-progression">17</a>, with the original cell cutoff and normalization, replaces each top prime by a real log coordinate and a Haar-unit residue modulo $M_*$. Its density is the principal harmonic density times the correction in <a href="#eq:src57" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src57">[eq:src57]</a>. For an external integer the analogous replacement uses <a id="eq:arith-external-density"></a>$$\label{eq:arith-external-density}
                         e^{t-G}\varphi(t-G)\,dt$$ and uniform residues on all classes modulo $M_*$. Elementary counting of integers in a progression gives this replacement with error $e^{-G+O(L)}$ per interval and residue. The total ideal measure of each coordinate is bounded. A prime cell’s normalizing reciprocal costs only $\exp(O(L))$.

<a id="eq:arith-giant-error"></a>
We give a joint error estimate so that no conditional equidistribution is implicit. Partition both log cells using mesh $\eta_g=\exp(-e^{.015L})$ and freeze the smooth factor $\Xi$ in each box. All other tests at fixed small variables and frequencies are residue tests. The variation error, including the pointwise costs above, is bounded by $$\exp\bigl(-e^{.015L}+O(e^{.012L})+O_k(m)\bigr).$$ The logarithm of the total number of boxes and pairs of residue classes is $O(e^{.015L}+e^{.012L})$. Thus the absolute interval errors from the giant row of <a href="#eq:src57" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src57">[eq:src57]</a>, even summed over all these boxes and classes and multiplied by the pointwise bound, contribute at most $$\exp\bigl(-c e^{.018L}+O(e^{.015L})+O(e^{.012L})+O_k(m)\bigr).$$ The integer-coordinate errors have the still stronger negative term $-G$. It follows that the whole giant replacement has uniform error $$\label{eq:arith-giant-error}
                         O\bigl(\exp(-e^{.013L})\bigr).$$ The Page factors are part of the measures in this argument; they need not be frozen or differentiated.

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
#### Integration of the actual and internal small-prime coordinates

<a id="eq:src58"></a>
In the ideal giant distribution, CRT separates its coordinates, and a surviving Page factor uses only $Q_f$. First integrate the giant coordinates modulo $c_{\rm sm}$. In the diagonal environment, $x_-$ is already a unit there, and $x_+$ must be restricted from all classes to units. At $\ell\mid H_{\rm sm}$ the argument of $g_\ell$ in <a href="#eq:src54" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src54">[eq:src54]</a> is a fixed unit divided by $x_+x_-$. It is therefore uniform on the unit group once both giants are units. Since $g_\ell(0)=0$ and $\mathbb E_{a\bmod\ell}|g_\ell(a)|^2=1$, the average of its squared modulus on units is $\ell/(\ell-1)$. Independence over $\ell$ gives $$\label{eq:src58}
 \frac{\phi(c_{\rm sm})}{c_{\rm sm}}
    \prod_{\ell\mid H_{\rm sm}}\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}
       =\prod_{\ell\mid u_{\rm out}}\left(1-\frac1\ell\right),$$ where $u_{\rm out}$ is the actual outer compensation product of type $j$ in the diagonal environment. This factor is bounded by one and is independent of the bulk values. In the final environment the analogous integral equals one. The support descent proved above shows that no remaining test uses these giant coordinates modulo the actual regular small primes.

Fix now a distinct internal sampled prime $b$. Each of its occurrences requires a numerator in <a href="#eq:arith-numerator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-numerator">[eq:arith-numerator]</a> to vanish modulo $b$. This numerator is a nonzero linear form in $(x_+,x_-)$ modulo $b$. To see nonvanishing, along its ancestor path each substitution retains one giant entry and replaces the other by a linear combination with two unit coefficients. Its matrix is invertible modulo $b$, and the current numerator is itself a nonzero row applied to this pair. All coefficients are rational expressions in other small regular or internal primes and in the fixed frequencies. No variable of the same compensation type as $b$ is needed: that type has not yet appeared at any ancestor, and the current numerator does not contain the current $u$. This also holds across the two histories, and the ancestor transformations are invertible modulo $b^2$.

<a id="eq:arith-line-probabilities"></a>
If the forms for all occurrences of $b$ have rank two, they have no solution under the sampled giant residues, because $x_-$ is a unit. In rank one, their common line has probability $$\label{eq:arith-line-probabilities}
 \begin{cases}
 (b-1)^{-1},&\text{both giants prime and both line coefficients nonzero},\\
 b^{-1},&\text{$x_+$ external and its coefficient nonzero},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ For example, in the second case each of the $b-1$ unit choices of $x_-$ determines one of the $b$ choices of $x_+$. These probabilities multiply over distinct $b$ by CRT.

<a id="eq:src59"></a>
Conditional on a rank-one solution modulo $b$, a forbidden zero modulo $b^2$ from <a href="#eq:arith-internal-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-internal-tests">[eq:arith-internal-tests]</a> removes at most $1/b$ of the uniform lifts: at least one coefficient of the form is a unit. A union bound over its occurrences gives a relative loss $O_k(1/b)$. To justify dropping these exclusions in the weighted expression, we first bound those weights. For a prime in a compensation log cell, every occurrence prior $\mu_i$ satisfies $b\mu_i(b)\leq e^{CL}$, with $C$ independent of $k$ once $L$ is sufficiently large. Thus, selecting any occurrence as representative, $$\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n_b}\mu_i(b)\right)
       \frac{b^{n_b}}{b-1}
 \leq 2\mu_1(b)e^{C(n_b-1)L}.$$ Summing over the $O(kr)$ occurrences shows that the joint measure after integrating these line conditions is dominated by independent representative priors times $$\label{eq:src59}
                              \exp(CkrL).$$ Restrictions that representatives be distinct can be discarded for this upper bound. The same domination holds in the external-integer case since $1/b\leq1/(b-1)$.

<a id="eq:arith-spectator-cost"></a>
After <a href="#eq:src58" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src58">[eq:src58]</a>, the remaining large pointwise factor is the spectator product. Since there are $2r$ leaf transforms at each spectator, its logarithm is at most $$\label{eq:arith-spectator-cost}
                              O_k(m e^{.001L}).$$ Every internal $b$ satisfies $\log b\asymp_k e^{.01L}$. The relative $O_k(1/b)$ losses, multiplied by <a href="#eq:src59" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src59">[eq:src59]</a>, <a href="#eq:arith-spectator-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-spectator-cost">[eq:arith-spectator-cost]</a>, and all $\exp(O_k(m))$ costs, are therefore negligible. We drop all the nonvanishing-modulo-$b^2$ exclusions henceforth.

<a id="pm-h-54"></a>
#### Removing arithmetic coincidences in the line conditions

For the signed comparison <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a> we need to remove dependence of the line probabilities on accidental bulk congruences. Fix the equality pattern among internal samples. Regard the remaining small-prime representatives and actual small primes as independent formal variables. For each $b$, clear the denominators in the coefficient-zero and two-by-two minor-zero tests for its forms. Those denominators are units modulo $b$. We obtain integer polynomials in the fixed frequencies and the formal variables, with no spectator variable and no variable of $b$’s compensation type.

<a id="eq:arith-polynomial-size"></a>
The degree is $O_k(m)$. This follows inductively because each coefficient in a reversal is a product of a subtree’s small slots times a frequency, and there are only $k$ substitutions on any path. Clearing the products of ancestor denominators and forming a two-by-two minor preserves this bound. The same induction gives, whenever an evaluation is nonzero, $$\label{eq:arith-polynomial-size}
                               \log|P|\leq e^{.012L}$$ for large $L$: the total degrees are $O_k(m)$, all small-prime logarithms are $O_k(e^{.01L})$, and the logarithms of the frequency coefficients are $O_k(m)$.

<a id="eq:arith-polynomial-root-bound"></a>
Replace each test by the question whether its polynomial is identically zero over $\mathbb Q$. We quantify the error in this replacement under <a href="#eq:src59" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src59">[eq:src59]</a>. If a polynomial of total degree $D$ in independent variables is not identically zero and each variable has maximal atom at most $\alpha$, then $$\label{eq:arith-polynomial-root-bound}
                             \mathbb P(P=0)\leq D\alpha.$$ This is a maximal-atom variant of the Schwartz–Zippel argument. Schwartz (Schwartz 1980, Lemma 1, p. 702) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[Schwartz1980](#ref-Schwartz1980)] gives the sharp total-degree estimate for uniform finite sets; Zippel (Zippel 1979, sec. 3.1, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[Zippel1979](#ref-Zippel1979)] gives related coordinate-degree zero estimates in sparse interpolation. Earlier random-evaluation identity testing appears in DeMillo and Lipton (DeMillo and Lipton 1978) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[DeMilloLipton1978](#ref-DeMilloLipton1978)]. The proof below extends the uniform-finite-set argument to independent nonuniform laws; that extension is supplied here, not imported from these references. Indeed, write it as a polynomial of degree $d$ in its last variable, with nonzero leading coefficient of degree at most $D-d$. Induction bounds the probability that this coefficient vanishes by $(D-d)\alpha$; otherwise there are at most $d$ roots in the last variable. This proves <a href="#eq:arith-polynomial-root-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-polynomial-root-bound">[eq:arith-polynomial-root-bound]</a>.

All variables occurring here are at least bulk size. Their maximal atoms are at most $\exp(-c e^{.004L})$, so an accidental exact zero has probability at most $O_k(m)\exp(-c e^{.004L})$. If the evaluation is nonzero, <a href="#eq:arith-polynomial-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-polynomial-size">[eq:arith-polynomial-size]</a> implies that it has at most $e^{O(L)}$ distinct prime divisors. The representative $b$ is independent of this evaluation, because its whole compensation type is absent from the polynomial. Its maximal atom is at most $\exp(-c_k e^{.01L}+O(L))$, so the probability that it divides this nonzero evaluation is again negligible. A polynomial which is an identity vanishes modulo $b$ identically, because the cleared denominators are units.

<a id="eq:arith-symbolic-error"></a>
The error bounds remain valid with the other support restrictions: for this purpose discard those restrictions and use the representative-prior domination. Both the original and the replaced line factors are bounded by $1/(b-1)$, so the same domination applies to their difference. There are only $O_k(1)$ tests, and multiplying by the spectator bound <a href="#eq:arith-spectator-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-spectator-cost">[eq:arith-spectator-cost]</a> and all $\exp(O_k(m))$ factors still gives an error $$\label{eq:arith-symbolic-error}
                              O\bigl(\exp(-e^{.002L})\bigr).$$

We now integrate out the giant coordinates modulo each internal $b$. For <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a> their contribution is its symbolic rank and feasibility flag times the appropriate scalar in <a href="#eq:arith-line-probabilities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-line-probabilities">[eq:arith-line-probabilities]</a>. The flags depend on the formal pattern and frequencies, not on the sampled bulk values. The line itself leaves no further condition, since no other factor uses the giant coordinates at $b$ after its $b^2$ exclusions have been removed. Thus replacing the flags and integrating the line conditions removes all dependence on bulk residues modulo internal primes.

For <a href="#eq:src55" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src55">[eq:src55]</a> there is a simpler nonnegative upper bound. Take absolute values in the two-history expansion and replace every line probability by its upper bound $1/(b-1)$ before using <a href="#eq:src59" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src59">[eq:src59]</a>. In this estimate rank and feasibility flags are discarded; they do not remain as restrictions on the bulk variables. The symbolic replacement is needed for the signed comparison, but not for this upper bound.

We may also remove distinctness among the actual bulk samples in the simplified expressions. There are $rm=O_k(m)$ such samples, and their collision probability under independent priors is at most their number of pairs times their maximal point mass. Equations <a href="#eq:src59" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src59">[eq:src59]</a> and <a href="#eq:arith-spectator-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-spectator-cost">[eq:arith-spectator-cost]</a> show that the resulting error is bounded by <a href="#eq:arith-symbolic-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-symbolic-error">[eq:arith-symbolic-error]</a>. This step extends the already simplified formula; it does not attempt to use <a href="#eq:src58" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src58">[eq:src58]</a> with a nonsquarefree $c_{\rm sm}$. Keep spectator distinctness and any restrictions involving only nonbulk variables.

<a id="pm-h-55"></a>
#### Joint idealization of the bulk variables

The remaining dependence on actual bulk values is smooth in their log coordinates or occurs modulo $dQ_f$. No condition involving their residues at internal sampled primes or at actual regular small primes remains. Furthermore <a id="eq:arith-bulk-modulus"></a>$$\label{eq:arith-bulk-modulus}
              \log(dQ_f)=O_k(m e^{.001L})<e^{.0012L}.$$ Use the bulk row of Lemma <a href="#lem:arith-progression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arith-progression">17</a> to replace the $rm$ bulk priors by their real harmonic densities and Haar-unit residues modulo $dQ_f$, retaining the possible Page corrections. The actual range $e^{.004L}\leq\log p\leq e^{.006L}$ is strictly inside the row’s permitted range. Its harmonic normalization is of order $L$. If a deleted prime lies in the bulk range, removing its single atom has negligible cost by the same maximal-atom estimate just used. Thus the approximation may use the all-prime formula, while keeping the given normalization constants.

<a id="eq:arith-bulk-box-count"></a>
<a id="eq:arith-bulk-error"></a>
Here too the approximation is joint. Let $N_b=rm$ and partition every bulk log range using mesh $\eta_b=\exp(-e^{.0014L})$. The logarithm of the number of joint boxes and joint residue classes is at most $$\label{eq:arith-bulk-box-count}
 O_k\bigl(m(e^{.0014L}+L+\log(dQ_f))\bigr).$$ Freeze the smooth weight in each box. Equations <a href="#eq:src59" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src59">[eq:src59]</a> and <a href="#eq:arith-spectator-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-spectator-cost">[eq:arith-spectator-cost]</a> bound its remaining total or pointwise cost by $\exp(O_k(m e^{.001L}))$. The derivative bound gives variation error at most $$\exp\bigl(-e^{.0014L}+O_k(m e^{.001L})+O_k(m)\bigr).$$ For the interval errors, even summing over all the boxes and residues in <a href="#eq:arith-bulk-box-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-bulk-box-count">[eq:arith-bulk-box-count]</a> gives the upper bound $$\exp\bigl(-c e^{.0016L}
       +O_k(m(e^{.0014L}+L+e^{.0012L}))
       +O_k(m e^{.001L})\bigr).$$ Both are $$\label{eq:arith-bulk-error}
                              O\bigl(\exp(-e^{.00125L})\bigr).$$ The bounded masses of the other coordinates add only $\exp(O_k(m))$. In the main term their real distributions are integrated as measures; the box count is charged only to the approximation errors. There is therefore no box-count factor multiplying a main term.

For fixed $k$ the number of frequency histories is $\exp(O_k(m))$, because each frequency has bound $\exp(O_k(m))$ and there are $O_k(1)$ nodes. The number of equality patterns is $O_k(1)$. Thus <a href="#eq:arith-giant-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-giant-error">[eq:arith-giant-error]</a>, <a href="#eq:arith-symbolic-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-symbolic-error">[eq:arith-symbolic-error]</a>, and <a href="#eq:arith-bulk-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-bulk-error">[eq:arith-bulk-error]</a> remain negligible after the frequency and pattern sums. These errors are uniform for each comparison of assignments. All surviving Page multipliers are functions only of the real and $Q_f$ coordinates and are bounded by two per sampled prime.

<a id="pm-h-56"></a>
#### The signed comparison

We prove <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a>. Condition in the resulting main expression on the real coordinates, the $Q_f$ coordinates, the nonbulk prime values, and the top-giant residues. The bulk residues on $d$ are then independent Haar units. The only remaining factor using them is <a href="#eq:arith-spectator-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-spectator-product">[eq:arith-spectator-product]</a>. In particular the support descent, <a href="#eq:src58" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src58">[eq:src58]</a>, and the integrated symbolic line factors have left no additional bulk condition at the other small primes.

At a fixed $q\mid d$, each bulk slot is an independent Haar unit. In either diagram its leaf products are marginally independent Haar units, because the leaves use disjoint nonempty sets of slots. Jointly, their law is exactly uniform on the subgroup specified by equality of the products in corresponding overlap components. To verify this, regard each bulk slot as an edge of the bipartite overlap multigraph. Its value contributes to the product at both endpoints. In each connected component the product of the left vertex products must equal the product of the right vertex products. Conversely, prescribe vertex products satisfying this relation, set the non-tree edges to one, and choose a spanning tree. Successively eliminate a terminal vertex by assigning its incident tree edge the value needed for that vertex. The last vertex is satisfied by the product relation. This proves surjectivity onto exactly the asserted subgroup. A homomorphism of finite groups sends the uniform distribution to the uniform distribution on its image, proving the claimed joint law.

All frequencies and nonbulk constants needed for Lemma <a href="#lem:tree-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tree-comparison">15</a> are units at $q$, and the ancestor and child relations are those of <a href="#eq:src36" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src36">[eq:src36]</a>. The selected spectator primes satisfy <a href="#eq:src35" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src35">[eq:src35]</a> uniformly, with a bound tending to zero; their sizes tend to infinity uniformly as well. Therefore <a href="#eq:src38" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src38">[eq:src38]</a> provides a bound $\epsilon_k(L)$, where $\epsilon_k(L)\to0$, for the absolute correlation at every spectator, uniformly in the conditioned data. By CRT the bulk coordinates at distinct spectators are independent, so their combined bound is $\epsilon_k(L)^m$.

The remaining total costs, including the representative domination, bounded masses, and all frequency sums, are $\exp(O_k(m))$. Consequently $$\left|\mathbb E\sum_s\mathcal G K_{\rm ar}
                   A_l^{(1)}(s)\overline{A_l^{(2)}(s)}\right|
 \leq \epsilon_k(L)^m\exp(O_k(m))
       +O\bigl(\exp(-e^{.00125L})\bigr)
 =\exp(-\omega_k(m)),$$ as required in <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-57"></a>
#### The norm comparison and the frequency sums

We finally prove <a href="#eq:src55" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src55">[eq:src55]</a>. Use the nonnegative upper bound described above. At the $d$ coordinates, <a href="#eq:src37" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src37">[eq:src37]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz bound the expectation of the absolute product of two tree values by $3^r$ for each spectator, hence by $3^{rm}$ in total. Retain the frequency divisibility conditions, and discard the Page multipliers at cost $2^{rm+O(1)}$. The bulk coordinates modulo $Q_f$ are now independent Haar units for the upper bound.

Because the assignments agree, the bulk slots split into exactly the same subtrees in the two histories. Fix the other necessary residues. Expose the product of all bulk slots and then expose the products on successive subtree splits, proceeding from the root downwards. Conditional on a parent product, the product $M_+$ in one child is uniform on the unit group modulo $Q_f$, and $M_-$ is determined by $M_+M_-$. This follows directly from the product map on two disjoint collections of independent Haar units. Ancestor pivots in both histories depend only on previously exposed splits and are known with the precision supplied by <a href="#eq:arith-frequency-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-frequency-modulus">[eq:arith-frequency-modulus]</a>.

We evaluate support indicators under this original Haar measure, without first conditioning on the validity of the complete histories. All frequencies are fixed at the outset. A current giant’s unit tests against descendant frequencies therefore depend only on already exposed data. Tests on a newly created pivot are evaluated after the split that creates it; tests on later pivots are deferred to their own splits, or discarded for this nonnegative upper bound. Thus the retained earlier tests do not select an unexposed split.

<a id="eq:arith-square-congruence"></a>
At a node, the support requires $X_\pm C_\pm$ to be units modulo its frequency. If that unit test fails we stop that branch, whose contribution is zero. Otherwise the condition $$\label{eq:arith-square-congruence}
 vX_-C_-M_-\equiv wX_+C_+M_+\pmod{|s|}$$ requires $(v,s)=(w,s)$, since all the other factors are units. Put $g=(v,w,s)$. In a soluble case, division by $g$ makes both frequency coefficients units modulo <a id="eq:arith-reduced-modulus"></a>$$\label{eq:arith-reduced-modulus}
                                a=\frac{|s|}{(v,w,s)}.$$ Substituting the fixed parent product $M_+M_-$ into <a href="#eq:arith-square-congruence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-square-congruence">[eq:arith-square-congruence]</a> then fixes $M_+^2$ to a specified unit modulo $a$. The other history at the same node fixes a square modulo $a'=|s'|/(v',w',s')$.

<a id="eq:arith-split-probability"></a>
For each odd prime power, a unit has at most two square roots, and for a power of two it has at most four. Thus the simultaneous square equations have at most $2^{\omega([a,a'])+1}$ solutions modulo $[a,a']$, where brackets denote the least common multiple. If they are inconsistent, their probability is zero. The ordinary divisor bound and $n/\phi(n)\leq\tau(n)$ give, uniformly for the moduli at hand, $$2^{\omega(n)+1}\frac{n}{\phi(n)}=\exp(o_k(m)),
             \qquad n\leq\exp(O_k(m)).$$ It follows that the conditional probability at this split is at most $$\label{eq:arith-split-probability}
                          \frac{\exp(o_k(m))}{[a,a']}.$$ Reduction of Haar units modulo $Q_f$ to this modulus is uniform. All earlier tests are measurable with respect to the earlier exposed products, so these conditional upper bounds multiply along the tree. Failed tests contribute zero and do not alter the upper bound.

<a id="eq:arith-lcm-sum"></a>
It remains to sum <a href="#eq:arith-split-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-split-probability">[eq:arith-split-probability]</a> over internal frequencies. The requisite numerical estimate is $$\label{eq:arith-lcm-sum}
                  \sum_{a,a'\leq V}\frac1{[a,a']}
                           \ll(1+\log V)^3.$$ Indeed, write $a=du$, $a'=dv$ with $(u,v)=1$, and then discard the coprimality condition. The left side is at most $$\sum_{d\leq V}\frac1d
                       \left(\sum_{u\leq V/d}\frac1u\right)^2,$$ which proves <a href="#eq:arith-lcm-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-lcm-sum">[eq:arith-lcm-sum]</a>. With child frequencies $v,w$ fixed, every $s$ giving a specified $a=|s|/(v,w,s)$ is of the form $s=\pm ad$ with $d\mid(v,w)$. Its multiplicity is therefore at most $2\tau((v,w))$. The analogous bound holds in the other history. Since these gcds and all the frequency bounds are at most $\exp(O_k(m))$, <a href="#eq:arith-lcm-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-lcm-sum">[eq:arith-lcm-sum]</a> shows that each pair of internal frequencies sums to $\exp(o_k(m))$, uniformly in the fixed child frequencies.

Drop the equality of the two root frequencies if necessary for this nonnegative numerical bound. Sum the root pair first while holding the children fixed, then proceed down the tree. Only the $2r$ leaf frequencies remain, giving at most $(2V_0)^{2r}$ choices. Combining this with <a href="#eq:arith-xi-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arith-xi-bound">[eq:arith-xi-bound]</a> and $E_0=\Delta_0+\sqrt m$ yields <a id="eq:arith-leaf-budget"></a>$$\label{eq:arith-leaf-budget}
 (2V_0)^{2r}e^{-r\Delta_0+O_k(1)}
                         =\exp(r\Delta_0+o_k(m)).$$ All the other losses fit $Crm+o_k(m)$ in the exponent. These are the $3^{rm}$ spectator bound, bounded per-coordinate masses and Page factors, the domination <a href="#eq:src59" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src59">[eq:src59]</a>, and $O_k(1)$ equality patterns. The constant can be absolute: the internal-sample count is $O(kr)$, and $kL/m\asymp k^{-3}$; fixed-$k$ constants not proportional to $m$ are absorbed by $o_k(m)$. Inserting the negligible approximation errors gives $$\mathbb E\sum_s\mathcal G|A_l(s)|^2
                    \leq\exp\bigl(r(\Delta_0+Cm)+o_k(m)\bigr).$$ This is <a href="#eq:src55" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src55">[eq:src55]</a>, and completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:comparisons" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparisons">16</a>.

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Completion of the proof

We now use Proposition <a href="#prop:comparisons" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparisons">16</a> to control the diagonals in the transfers and then symmetrize the final amplitude. All parameters, priors, and zero conventions are those of Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">7</a>. In particular, the constants denoted by $C$ in costs $Crm$ are independent of the depth $k$ and of the large gap constants. The limits are taken with all these constants and $k$ fixed, and then $L\to\infty$.

<a id="pm-h-59"></a>
#### Counting bad arrangements

<a id="eq:src60"></a>
At level $l$ put $r=2^l$. Call a pair of arrangements of the $rm$ bulk variables *bad* if it is not covered by <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a>. Thus every pair is called bad when $l=0,1$; when $l\ge2$, a bad pair has more than $3r/4$ connected components in its bipartite overlap graph. For each fixed first arrangement, the number of bad second arrangements satisfies $$\label{eq:src60}
 N_{\mathrm{bad}}(r,m)
 \le (m!)^r
       \exp\bigl((\tfrac14\log r+C)rm+O_k(1)\bigr).$$ Here arrangements distinguish all bulk positions and all sampled bulk variables, so they are indexed by permutations even before any distinctness restrictions are imposed.

To prove <a href="#eq:src60" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src60">[eq:src60]</a> for $l\ge2$, write $a_i$ for the number of leaves on either side of the $i$th connected component. The numbers on the two sides agree: every vertex has degree $m$, and counting the component’s edges on either side gives the equality. If there are $t$ components, then $$\sum_{i=1}^t a_i=r,
 \qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^t(a_i-1)=r-t<\frac r4.$$ There are only $\exp(O_k(1))$ ways to choose and pair the two partitions of the leaf sets into these components. Once they are chosen, the variables incident to a component can be assigned to its second set of slots in at most $(a_i m)!$ ways. For $1\le a\le r$, $$a\log a
  =(a-1)\log a+\log a
  \le(a-1)(\log r+1),
 \qquad
 (am)!\le(m!)^a a^{am}.$$ The factorial inequality follows by bounding one multinomial coefficient by the sum of all multinomial coefficients with $a$ parts. Consequently $$\prod_i(a_i m)!
 \le(m!)^r\exp\left(m\sum_i a_i\log a_i\right)
 \le(m!)^r\exp\left(\frac14(\log r+1)rm\right),$$ which proves the claim. For $r=1,2$, the upper bound $(rm)!\le(m!)^r r^{rm}$ proves the same assertion after enlarging the absolute constant $C$. Nonbulk matchings in a diagonal have only $\exp(O_k(1))$ possibilities, since their number of slots is fixed once $k$ is fixed.

<a id="eq:conclusion-bad-fraction"></a>
We shall also use the corresponding bound for the fraction of bad arrangements. Since $m!\le m^m$ and $(rm)!\ge(rm/e)^{rm}$, $$\label{eq:conclusion-bad-fraction}
 \frac{N_{\mathrm{bad}}(r,m)}{(rm)!}
 \le\exp\bigl((-\tfrac34\log r+C)rm+O_k(1)\bigr).$$

<a id="pm-h-60"></a>
#### Diagonal bounds and the order of parameter choices

Let $\mathcal D_j$ be the nonnegative diagonal contribution in the extended square at step $j$, before the factor $e^{c_g}$ in <a href="#eq:src53" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src53">[eq:src53]</a>. At this step the two compared amplitudes have level $j-1$ and $r_j=2^{j-1}$ leaves. We first estimate the bad matchings.

<a id="eq:conclusion-diagonal-total"></a>
For each such matching, apply $|A^{(1)}\overline{A^{(2)}}|\le
(|A^{(1)}|^2+|A^{(2)}|^2)/2$ on their simultaneous support, after the nonnegative-square reduction that gives <a href="#eq:src54" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src54">[eq:src54]</a>. For either retained square, extract the point probability of the *other* ordered tuple. On this support it is at most $$C_H\exp(-T_H+O_k(1)),
 \qquad
 C_H\le L^{-r_jm}\exp(Cr_jm+O_k(1)).$$ The retained tuple is then summed with its own priors. This order of extraction is valid for both squares, including when a matching exchanges positions with different log-cell priors. The external integer measure and the $u$ weight contribute $\exp(G+r_jw_j)$; by <a href="#eq:src48" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src48">[eq:src48]</a>, their combination with $\exp(-T_H)$ is $\exp(-\Delta_j)$. All remaining Archimedean factors are bounded by $\exp(O_k(1))$ on the simultaneous support. Thus the one-assignment estimate <a href="#eq:src55" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src55">[eq:src55]</a>, followed by <a href="#eq:src60" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src60">[eq:src60]</a>, bounds the bad part by $$\exp(-\Delta_j) L^{-r_jm}(m!)^{r_j}
 \exp\bigl(r_j\Delta_0+
       (\tfrac14\log r_j+C)r_jm+o_k(m)\bigr).$$ Since $m/L\le z$, we have $L^{-r_jm}(m!)^{r_j}\le\exp(r_jm\log z)$. We obtain <a id="eq:src61"></a>$$\label{eq:src61}
 \mathcal D_{j,\mathrm{bad}}
 \le \exp\left(
 -\Delta_j+r_j\bigl\{\Delta_0+
       (\log z+\tfrac14\log r_j+C)m\bigr\}+o_k(m)
 \right).$$ For every other matching, <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a> applies with its stated Archimedean multiplier. After the probability extraction, the number and size of these terms cost at most $\exp(O_k(m))$: in particular, $$(r_jm)!L^{-r_jm}
 \le\exp\bigl(r_jm\log(r_jm/L)\bigr)
 =\exp(O_k(m)).$$ Their total is therefore $\exp(-\omega_k(m))$. In this notation, $\omega_k(m)/m\to\infty$ at every fixed $k$. Combining the two parts, $$\label{eq:conclusion-diagonal-total}
 \mathcal D_j\le
 \exp\left(
 -\Delta_j+r_j\bigl\{\Delta_0+
       (\log z+\tfrac14\log r_j+C)m\bigr\}+o_k(m)
 \right)+\exp(-\omega_k(m)).$$

<a id="eq:conclusion-budget"></a>
We specify the parameter order and the reserve in the iteration. The constant $B_s$ has already been chosen sufficiently large for <a href="#eq:src47" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src47">[eq:src47]</a>, whose constant $B_*$ is independent of the later gaps and of $k$. Write $$Z_G=\sum_{p\ \mathrm{prime}}
             \frac{\varphi(\log p-G)}p,
 \qquad c_g=\log Z_G^{-1}.$$ The giant range and the prime-cell normalization give $Z_G\sim G^{-1}$ and $c_g\le .91L$ for all sufficiently large $L$, with the coefficient $.91$ independent of all the gap choices and $k$. Fix, now, $$\label{eq:conclusion-budget}
 B=B_*+3.$$ Choose $B_z\ge9$ and then choose $B_D$ so large that <a id="eq:conclusion-gap-choice"></a>$$\label{eq:conclusion-gap-choice}
 B_D\ge B_s+2B+C+6,$$ where $C$ dominates the uniform constant in <a href="#eq:conclusion-diagonal-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-diagonal-total">[eq:conclusion-diagonal-total]</a>. These are fixed constants, chosen before $k$.

<a id="eq:conclusion-diagonal-reserve"></a>
<a id="eq:conclusion-cg-reserve"></a>
Indeed, substitution from <a href="#eq:src43" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src43">[eq:src43]</a> shows that the exponent of the first term in <a href="#eq:conclusion-diagonal-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-diagonal-total">[eq:conclusion-diagonal-total]</a>, divided by $r_jm$, is <a id="eq:conclusion-diagonal-rate"></a>$$\label{eq:conclusion-diagonal-rate}
 B_s-B_D+(9-B_z)\log z
       -\frac3{20}\log r_j+C+o_k(1).$$ Hence, for every sufficiently large fixed $k$ and then sufficiently large $L$, uniformly for $1\le j\le k$, $$\label{eq:conclusion-diagonal-reserve}
 \mathcal D_j\le\exp\bigl(-(2B+3)r_jm\bigr).$$ The precise choice of $k$ will be made below. We require already that it be large enough that, using $m=k^4L+O(1)$, $$\label{eq:conclusion-cg-reserve}
 \frac{c_g+\log2}{m}\le\frac2{k^4}<1$$ for all sufficiently large $L$.

<a id="eq:conclusion-transfer-lower"></a>
<a id="eq:conclusion-exact-recurrence"></a>
The transfer identity underlying <a href="#eq:src53" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src53">[eq:src53]</a> gives $$e^{-c_g}|\eta_{j-1}|^2
 \le\mathcal D_j+\eta_j
 \le\mathcal D_j+|\eta_j|.$$ If $|\eta_{j-1}|\ge\exp(-Br_jm)$, then <a href="#eq:conclusion-diagonal-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-diagonal-reserve">[eq:conclusion-diagonal-reserve]</a> and <a href="#eq:conclusion-cg-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-cg-reserve">[eq:conclusion-cg-reserve]</a> imply $\mathcal D_j\le\tfrac12 e^{-c_g}|\eta_{j-1}|^2$. Therefore $$\label{eq:conclusion-transfer-lower}
 |\eta_j|\ge\frac12e^{-c_g}|\eta_{j-1}|^2.$$ Starting with <a href="#eq:src47" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src47">[eq:src47]</a>, induction proves simultaneously this estimate and the sharper bound $$\label{eq:conclusion-exact-recurrence}
 -\log|\eta_j|
 \le 2^jB_*m+(2^j-1)(c_g+\log2)
 <B2^jm,
 \qquad 0\le j\le k.$$ For the induction, the sharper bound at $j-1$ supplies the coarse hypothesis needed for <a href="#eq:conclusion-transfer-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-transfer-lower">[eq:conclusion-transfer-lower]</a>; that inequality then gives the displayed sharper bound at $j$. Its final inequality follows from <a href="#eq:conclusion-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-budget">[eq:conclusion-budget]</a> and <a href="#eq:conclusion-cg-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-cg-reserve">[eq:conclusion-cg-reserve]</a>. In particular the reserve is inherited from the initial estimate rather than spent afresh at each step.

The transfers have now retained an exponential lower bound for $|\eta_k|$. We will obtain the opposite bound by averaging $A_k$ over the bulk arrangements. Since $G_R(s/d)$ depends only on their prime values, not their positions, it is common to these arrangements. The Cauchy–Schwarz step therefore also requires a norm estimate for this regular-transform factor.

<a id="pm-h-61"></a>
#### The norm of the common regular transform

<a id="eq:conclusion-regular-norm"></a>
At the final level put $r=2^k$. The sum over $s$ in this subsection is over the nonzero signed integers with $|s|\le V_k$. We claim $$\label{eq:conclusion-regular-norm}
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf d,\mathcal I_k}
       \sum_{0<|s|\le V_k}|G_R(s/d)|^2\ll V_k,$$ with an absolute implied constant for sufficiently large $L$ at the fixed parameters. Only the pairwise-distinctness and coprimality zero conventions of $G_R$ are needed here; bins and all history restrictions belong to the amplitudes.

Fix $s$, the spectator list $\mathbf d$, the actual regular small primes, and one of the two giant primes, denoted by $q$. Configurations with a repeated regular small prime or with a regular small prime dividing $d$ already give zero and can be discarded. Let $C$ be the squarefree product of the remaining regular small primes, and call the other giant $p$. Both giant transforms have absolute value at most one. After dropping their squared absolute values, the remaining factor is <a id="eq:conclusion-residue-test"></a>$$\label{eq:conclusion-residue-test}
 F(p\bmod C),\qquad
 F(a)=\prod_{\ell\mid C}
       \left|g_\ell(a_\ell a^{-1}\bmod\ell)\right|^2,
 \qquad
 a_\ell=\frac{s}{dq(C/\ell)}\pmod\ell .$$ All $a_\ell$ are units: the retained primes are distinct and coprime to $d$, and $0<|s|\le V_k$ is smaller than every actual slot prime. The restriction $p\ne q$ may now be dropped, since the remaining nonnegative expression is still defined when $p=q$. The giant range is disjoint from all the small-prime ranges, so every prime $p$ in its cell is a unit modulo $C$.

<a id="eq:conclusion-crt-norm"></a>
Write $U_C=(\mathbb Z/C\mathbb Z)^\times$. By the Chinese remainder theorem, as $a$ varies uniformly over $U_C$, the arguments $a_\ell a^{-1}\pmod\ell$ are independent uniform nonzero residues. The exact local normalizations $g_\ell(0)=0$ and $\ell^{-1}\sum_x|g_\ell(x)|^2=1$ therefore give the identities $$\label{eq:conclusion-crt-norm}
 \sum_{a\in U_C}F(a)=C,
 \qquad
 \frac1{\phi(C)}\sum_{a\in U_C}F(a)
   =\frac C{\phi(C)}
   =\prod_{\ell\mid C}\frac\ell{\ell-1}.$$ Here $\phi$ is Euler’s totient. There are $O_k(m)$ factors, and every such prime is at least $\exp(\exp(.004L))$. Consequently $C/\phi(C)=1+o_k(1)$ uniformly in the fixed lists. This calculation uses no uniform pointwise bound on the small-prime transforms.

<a id="eq:conclusion-progression-average"></a>
We next justify the averaging over the actual prime $p$, including the possible exceptional term. The size bounds for the final list give $$\log C=O_k(h+m\exp(.006L))\le\exp(.012L)$$ for sufficiently large $L$, so the giant row of <a href="#eq:src57" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src57">[eq:src57]</a> applies with modulus $C$. With the positive constant $c$ from that estimate, put $$\varepsilon_L=\exp(-c\exp(.018L)).$$ Splitting the support of $\varphi(t-G)$ into intervals of length at most one and applying partial summation gives, uniformly for $a\in U_C$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\substack{p\ \mathrm{prime}\\p\equiv a\pmod C}}
       \frac{\varphi(\log p-G)}p
 &=\frac1{\phi(C)}\int
       \frac{\varphi(t-G)}t
       \bigl(1-\chi_*(a)e^{(\beta_*-1)t}\bigr)\,dt
       +O(\varepsilon_L).
       \label{eq:conclusion-progression-average}
\end{aligned}$$ As in <a href="#eq:src57" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src57">[eq:src57]</a>, the exceptional term is omitted if it is absent or its conductor does not divide $C$. When it is present, $$0\le1-\chi_*(a)e^{(\beta_*-1)t}\le2.$$ This pointwise inequality suffices even if the exceptional character correlates with $F$.

Multiply <a href="#eq:conclusion-progression-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-progression-average">[eq:conclusion-progression-average]</a> by $F(a)$, sum over $a\in U_C$, and divide by $Z_G$. Since $F\ge0$, the exact sum in <a href="#eq:conclusion-crt-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-crt-norm">[eq:conclusion-crt-norm]</a> both bounds the main term and sums the absolute progression errors. It follows that <a id="eq:conclusion-prime-norm"></a>$$\label{eq:conclusion-prime-norm}
 \mathbb E_p F(p\bmod C)
 \le
 2\frac C{\phi(C)}
       \frac{\displaystyle\int\varphi(t-G)\,dt/t}{Z_G}
       +O(Z_G^{-1}C\varepsilon_L)
 =2+o_k(1).$$ Indeed the integral divided by $Z_G$ is $1+o(1)$ by ordinary prime-cell normalization. Moreover $Z_G^{-1}=\exp(O(L))$ and $\log C\le\exp(.012L)$, whereas $\varepsilon_L=\exp(-c\exp(.018L))$, so the summed error is $o(1)$. All estimates are uniform in $s$, $\mathbf d$, $q$, and the retained small-prime list. Averaging those variables and summing over the at most $2V_k$ frequencies proves <a href="#eq:conclusion-regular-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-regular-norm">[eq:conclusion-regular-norm]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-62"></a>
#### Symmetrization and contradiction

Let $\mathfrak S_{rm}$ permute the values in all $rm$ bulk positions, keeping every other position fixed. The bulk priors are identical, including their allowed prime deletions, so their joint law is invariant under this action. Also $G_R(s/d)$ is unchanged: the product $R$, the set of its prime factors, the exact transform attached to each bulk prime value, and every argument $s/(d(R/\ell))$ are unchanged. Its distinctness and coprimality zero conventions are invariant as well. Define <a id="eq:conclusion-symmetrization"></a>$$\label{eq:conclusion-symmetrization}
 A_{\mathrm{sym}}(s)
   =\frac1{(rm)!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak S_{rm}}A_k^\pi(s),$$ where $A_k^\pi$ has its bulk values placed according to $\pi$. Changing variables separately for each permutation in <a href="#eq:src49" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src49">[eq:src49]</a> yields $$\eta_k=\mathbb E\sum_{0<|s|\le V_k}G_R(s/d)A_{\mathrm{sym}}(s).$$ In this operation all bins and history-dependent support conditions remain inside their respective $A_k^\pi$; they need not be invariant. Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:conclusion-regular-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-regular-norm">[eq:conclusion-regular-norm]</a> give <a id="eq:conclusion-cauchy-schwarz"></a>$$\label{eq:conclusion-cauchy-schwarz}
 |\eta_k|^2
 \le\left(\mathbb E\sum_s|G_R(s/d)|^2\right)
        \left(\mathbb E\sum_s|A_{\mathrm{sym}}(s)|^2\right)
 \ll V_k\mathbb E\sum_s|A_{\mathrm{sym}}(s)|^2.$$

<a id="eq:conclusion-final-upper"></a>
Expand the second factor into ordered pairs of permutations. Each bad pair is at most $\exp(r(\Delta_0+Cm)+o_k(m))$ in absolute value by Cauchy–Schwarz and the two one-assignment bounds <a href="#eq:src55" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src55">[eq:src55]</a>. Its fraction among all pairs is bounded by <a href="#eq:conclusion-bad-fraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-bad-fraction">[eq:conclusion-bad-fraction]</a>. Each remaining pair satisfies <a href="#eq:src56" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src56">[eq:src56]</a>; that estimate is uniform, so averaging these pairs preserves its bound. Consequently $$\label{eq:conclusion-final-upper}
 |\eta_k|^2\ll e^{E_k}
 \left[
 \exp\bigl(r\{\Delta_0+(-\tfrac34\log r+C)m\}+o_k(m)\bigr)
       +\exp(-\omega_k(m))
 \right].$$

<a id="eq:conclusion-final-rate"></a>
For completeness, the sums in <a href="#eq:src43" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:src43">[eq:src43]</a> satisfy $$\sum_{j=1}^k r_j=r-1,
 \qquad
 \sum_{j=1}^k r_j\log r_j
   =r\log r-2r\log2+2\log2.$$ They give, in particular, the convenient upper bound <a id="eq:conclusion-frequency-budget"></a>$$\label{eq:conclusion-frequency-budget}
 \frac{E_k}{rm}
 \le\frac25\log r+B_D+B_s+(B_z+8)\log z+o_k(1).$$ Since $\Delta_0/m=B_s+8\log z$, the logarithm of the first term on the right of <a href="#eq:conclusion-final-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-final-upper">[eq:conclusion-final-upper]</a>, divided by $rm$, is at most $$\label{eq:conclusion-final-rate}
 -\frac7{20}\log r+B_D+2B_s+(B_z+16)\log z+C+o_k(1).$$ All constants here have already been fixed. Now choose $k$ sufficiently large, retaining <a href="#eq:conclusion-cg-reserve" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-cg-reserve">[eq:conclusion-cg-reserve]</a>, that the expression in <a href="#eq:conclusion-final-rate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-final-rate">[eq:conclusion-final-rate]</a> without its $o_k(1)$ term is less than $-2B-4$. This is possible because $\log r=k\log2$ while $\log z=4\log k$. Finally take $L$ sufficiently large. The first term of <a href="#eq:conclusion-final-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-final-upper">[eq:conclusion-final-upper]</a> is then at most $\exp(-(2B+3)rm)$, after absorbing its absolute implied constant. The second term remains $\exp(-\omega_k(m))$, since $E_k=O_k(m)$. Thus, for sufficiently large $L$, $$|\eta_k|^2\le\exp(-(2B+1)rm).$$ This contradicts <a href="#eq:conclusion-exact-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-exact-recurrence">[eq:conclusion-exact-recurrence]</a>, which gives $|\eta_k|^2\ge\exp(-2Brm)$. The assumed decomposition is therefore impossible: no two infinite subsets of $\mathbb N_0$ have a sumset whose symmetric difference with the positive primes is finite. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">4</a>.
